 \le\langle\psi_i(a),(h_i(b)-h_i(a))\psi_i(a)\rangle.$$ Both bounding quantities differ from $-q'(f(b)-f(a))$ by the error just estimated, proving <a href="#eq:wells-own-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-own-slope">[eq:wells-own-slope]</a>.

For $k\ne i$, the moving secondary pole in $\chi_kV_k$ stays at least $D/32$ from the region where its cap is nonzero. Its bounded potential changes in sup norm by at most $Cq'D^{-2}|\rho_k-\widetilde\rho_k|$. Summing and applying the variational principle for a bounded additive perturbation proves <a href="#eq:wells-other-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-other-slope">[eq:wells-other-slope]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Uniform tails

<a id="stmt-lem-well-tail"></a>
<div id="lem:well-tail" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:wells-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:wells-h1-tail"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Crude tails before tuning). *Uniformly for all $\rho\in[1,4]^m$, $$|\psi_i(y)|\le C\exp[-.99(r_i-.01D)],
 \label{eq:wells-tail}$$ and, for sufficiently large $D$, $$\|\psi_i\|_{H^1(\{r_i>.65D\})}\le e^{-.61D}.
 \label{eq:wells-h1-tail}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-well-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-well-tail</summary>

*Proof.* On $r_i\ge .01D$, both own poles are far away and $V_i+W_i=O(m/D)$. For $a=.99$ and $b(y)=C\exp[-a(r_i-.01D)]$, direct calculation gives $$\frac{(h_i-\lambda_i)b}{b}
 =-\frac{a^2}{2}+\frac a{r_i}-V_i-W_i-\lambda_i.$$ Its constant part is $(1-a^2)/2+O(m/D)>0$ at the chosen scales. The zero-order coefficient of $h_i-\lambda_i$ is also positive throughout this exterior region. The uniform sup bound fixes $C$ so that $b\ge|\psi_i|$ at the inner boundary. An $H^2(\mathbb R^3)$ function is uniformly continuous and vanishes at infinity; otherwise uniform continuity on a sequence of disjoint balls would contradict its square integrability. On a large finite annulus add a constant $\varepsilon>0$ to $b$ to dominate both signs of $\psi_i$ at the outer boundary. The supersolution inequality is preserved. Testing the negative part of $b+\varepsilon\mp\psi_i$ proves comparison. Send the outer radius to infinity and then $\varepsilon$ to zero. Inside $.01D$, the same sup bound proves <a href="#eq:wells-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-tail">[eq:wells-tail]</a>.

For the gradient estimate choose a smooth cutoff $\zeta$ that vanishes on $r_i\le .645D$, equals one on $r_i\ge .65D$, and has gradient $O(D^{-1})$. Testing the eigen-equation with $\zeta^2\psi_i$ gives $$\frac12\|\nabla(\zeta\psi_i)\|_2^2
 =\int(V_i+W_i+\lambda_i)|\zeta\psi_i|^2\,\mathrm dy
       +\frac12\int|\nabla\zeta|^2|\psi_i|^2\,\mathrm dy.$$ All coefficients on this support are bounded. Thus <a href="#eq:wells-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-tail">[eq:wells-tail]</a> bounds the exterior $H^1$ norm by a polynomial in $D$ times $\exp[-.99(.645-.01)D]$. Since $.99(.645-.01)=.62865>.61$, the polynomial factor is absorbed for large $D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### An analytic estimate with uniform constants

We give the local regularity argument used in the numerical procedure. It explains why the shrinking length scales near a moving Coulomb pole do not increase the required polynomial degree beyond $O(D)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-wells-analytic"></a>
<div id="lem:wells-analytic" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:wells-analytic-coefficient"></a>
<a id="eq:wells-analytic-derivatives"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Uniform analytic patches). *Suppose $u$ is bounded on the unit ball in $\mathbb R^3$ and satisfies $\Delta u=Fu$ there, where $$\|\partial^\beta F\|_\infty\le A^{|\beta|+1}|\beta|!
 \quad\text{for every multi-index }\beta.
 \label{eq:wells-analytic-coefficient}$$ There is a constant $A_1$, depending only on $A$, such that $$\max_{|\alpha|=k}\|\partial^\alpha u\|_{L^\infty(B_{1/4})}
 \le \|u\|_{L^2(B_1)}A_1^{k+1}k!.
 \label{eq:wells-analytic-derivatives}$$ On a sufficiently small fixed ball about the origin, Taylor polynomials consequently have geometrically decreasing errors in both value and first derivative, with constants depending only on $A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-wells-analytic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-wells-analytic</summary>

<a id="eq:wells-local-h2"></a>
<a id="eq:wells-local-h1"></a>
*Proof.* For two concentric balls of radii between $1/2$ and $1$, separated by $h$, the local estimates needed below are $$\begin{aligned}
 \|D^2v\|_{2,\mathrm{inner}}
 &\le C\bigl(\|\Delta v\|_{2,\mathrm{outer}}
                   +h^{-2}\|v\|_{2,\mathrm{outer}}\bigr),
 \label{eq:wells-local-h2}\\
 \|\nabla v\|_{2,\mathrm{inner}}
 &\le C\bigl(h\|\Delta v\|_{2,\mathrm{outer}}
                   +h^{-1}\|v\|_{2,\mathrm{outer}}\bigr).
 \label{eq:wells-local-h1}
\end{aligned}$$ To obtain them, insert smooth cutoffs with first and second derivatives $O(h^{-1})$ and $O(h^{-2})$. Integration by parts against a squared cutoff and Cauchy–Schwarz, with $ab\le (h a)^2/2+(b/h)^2/2$, bound first derivatives on an intermediate ball as in <a href="#eq:wells-local-h1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-local-h1">[eq:wells-local-h1]</a>. Apply the whole-space Fourier inequality $\|D^2w\|_2\le C\|\Delta w\|_2$ to the cutoff function and substitute that first-derivative bound in its commutator terms to obtain <a href="#eq:wells-local-h2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-local-h2">[eq:wells-local-h2]</a>. Approximation proves these estimates for weak solutions. Successive localized Laplacian regularity makes $u$ smooth when $F$ is smooth, justifying the following differentiations.

Fix an integer $\ell\ge2$, set $r_j=1-j/(2\ell)$, and write $$T_j=\max_{|\alpha|=j}\|\partial^\alpha u\|_{L^2(B_{r_j})},
 \qquad M=\|u\|_{L^2(B_1)}.$$ The first-derivative bound gives $T_1\le C(\ell+A/\ell)M$. For $j\le\ell-2$, apply <a href="#eq:wells-local-h2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-local-h2">[eq:wells-local-h2]</a> to an order-$j$ derivative between $B_{r_j}$ and $B_{r_{j+2}}$. Leibniz’ rule yields <a id="eq:wells-analytic-recurrence"></a>$$T_{j+2}\le C\left(
  \sum_{b=0}^j\binom jb A^{b+1}b!\,T_{j-b}
  +\ell^2T_j\right).
 \label{eq:wells-analytic-recurrence}$$ Lower-order derivatives are available on the larger balls $B_{r_{j-b}}$. The multi-index coefficient sum of order $b$ is $\binom jb$, by the multinomial identity. If preceding orders satisfy $T_s\le M(K\ell)^s$, the sum is at most $$AM(K\ell)^j\sum_{b=0}^j(A/K)^b
 \le2AM(K\ell)^j$$ for $K\ge2A$, using $\binom jb b!\le\ell^b$. Increasing the fixed $K$ absorbs the remaining $C\ell^2$ factor into $(K\ell)^2$. Both parity chains therefore satisfy $T_j\le M(K\ell)^j$.

Set $\ell=k+2$. Sobolev embedding on fixed smaller balls bounds the sup norm of an order-$k$ derivative on $B_{1/4}$ by $CM[K(k+2)]^{k+2}$. The inequality $k!\ge(k/e)^k$, with the small cases absorbed in the constant, converts this into <a href="#eq:wells-analytic-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-analytic-derivatives">[eq:wells-analytic-derivatives]</a>. Taylor’s formula, summed over multi-indices, bounds the degree-$L$ remainder on $|z|\le a$ by a constant times $(C_1a)^{L+1}$. The first-derivative remainder has an additional factor polynomial in $L$ and the same geometric decay. Fixing $a$ small enough that $C_1a<1$ proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### A polynomial-size graded Ritz space

Graded meshes with increasing polynomial degree are a standard way to combine singular local behavior with exponential approximation away from the singularities. For related eigenvalue approximation results with singular potentials, see Maday and Marcati (Maday and Marcati 2019) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[MadayMarcati2019](#ref-MadayMarcati2019)]. Their bounded-domain discontinuous-Galerkin estimates are not an oracle for the present moving attractive Coulomb wells. We construct the needed conforming space and then certify its matrix arithmetic separately. More specifically, the products of tensor-linear hats with local polynomials below form a partition-of-unity approximation space, as in Melenk and Babuška (Melenk and Babuška 1996, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[MelenkBabuska1996](#ref-MelenkBabuska1996)]. We give the cutoff and derivative estimates explicitly because the tensor cells can have arbitrarily large aspect ratios.

This is a form-domain approximation: the piecewise polynomial functions need only belong to $H^1$. Their kinetic matrix uses first weak derivatives, not second derivatives across element boundaries.

<a id="stmt-lem-wells-ritz-approximation"></a>
<div id="lem:wells-ritz-approximation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:wells-ritz-h1"></a>
<a id="eq:wells-ritz-bias"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Exponentially accurate Ritz approximation). *For each $i$, each rational choice of centers and secondary parameters, and each sufficiently large $D$, one can explicitly construct a space $\mathcal V_i\subset H^1(\mathbb R^3)$ of rational piecewise polynomials, of dimension polynomial in $D$, supported in $r_i<.8D$, such that $$\inf_{v\in\mathcal V_i}\|v-\psi_i\|_{H^1}\le e^{-.58D}.
 \label{eq:wells-ritz-h1}$$ Its lowest Ritz value $\lambda_i^{\mathrm R}$ satisfies $$0\le\lambda_i^{\mathrm R}-\lambda_i\le C e^{-1.16D}.
 \label{eq:wells-ritz-bias}$$ The construction and constants are uniform over rational parameters throughout $[1,4]^m$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-wells-ritz-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-wells-ritz-approximation</summary>

<a id="eq:wells-grid-rule"></a>
*Proof.* Work in local coordinates $z=y-X_i$ on $[-D,D]^3$, with poles at $0$ and $\rho_i e_z$. Put $\delta=2^{-8D}$ and fix a sufficiently small rational $\xi>0$, as specified below. On each coordinate axis let $B$ be the set of projections of the two poles. Starting from $[-D,D]$, bisect dyadically until every interval $I$ obeys $$|I|\le\xi\min\{1,\operatorname{dist}(I,B)+\delta\}.
 \label{eq:wells-grid-rule}$$ Every leaf length is at least $\xi\delta/2$, since its parent failed the stopping rule. Above a fixed spacing there are $O(D)$ intervals. At a finer level of length $h$, only intervals within $h/\xi$ of one of the at most two projections can still split, so a fixed number of intervals per level are split there. There are $O(\log(D/\delta))=O(D+\log D)$ levels, hence $O(D)$ intervals per axis. This remains true for poles arbitrarily close to dyadic boundaries. The tensor product has $O(D^3)$ nodes and cells.

<a id="eq:wells-retained-nodes"></a>
Let $b_w$ be the tensor-linear hat at node $w$. The full hats form a partition of unity in the cube. Retain nodes satisfying $$|w|<.73D,\qquad |w|>\delta,\qquad
 |w-\rho_i e_z|>\delta,
 \label{eq:wells-retained-nodes}$$ and take $b_w(z)(z-w)^\alpha$ for all $|\alpha|\le L_0$, where $L_0$ is a sufficiently large fixed multiple of $D$. These functions span $\mathcal V_i$, whose dimension before removing dependencies is $O(D^6)$.

<a id="eq:wells-patch-radius"></a>
Here is the support estimate that avoids any aspect-ratio assumption. For either pole $a$, every coordinate interval adjacent to $w$ obeys $$|I|\le\xi\min\{1,|w_j-a_j|+\delta\}.$$ The support radius $R_w^{\mathrm{hat}}$ of a retained hat thus satisfies $$\begin{split}
 R_w^{\mathrm{hat}}
 &\le\xi\bigl(|w-a|+\sqrt3\delta\bigr)
   \le(1+\sqrt3)\xi|w-a|,\\
 R_w^{\mathrm{hat}}&\le\sqrt3\xi.
\end{split}
 \label{eq:wells-patch-radius}$$ For $d_w=\min\{1,|w|,|w-\rho_i e_z|\}$ this gives $R_w^{\mathrm{hat}}\le C\xi d_w$. Each support avoids both poles and, for large $D$, lies in $|z|<.8D$.

Choose a fixed $c<1/10$ and set $R=c d_w$. The whole ball $B(w,R)$ avoids both poles. Throughout it every remote primary has distance at least $D-2S-.73D-c>D/8$, so it also avoids every remote cap transition. On this enlarged ball the eigen-equation contains only pure Coulomb potentials. Rescaling by $R$ gives $\Delta u=Fu$ on the unit ball. Its coefficient satisfies <a href="#eq:wells-analytic-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-analytic-coefficient">[eq:wells-analytic-coefficient]</a> with an absolute $A$: local inverse-distance expansions give factorial derivative bounds because $R/\operatorname{dist}(w,a)\le c$; the extra $R^2$ in the coefficient makes $R^2/\operatorname{dist}(w,a)$ bounded; and remote contributions sum to at most $Cm/D$. The eigenvalue term is bounded too. The sup norm of $u$ is uniform by [prop:isolated-well](#prop:isolated-well).

<a id="eq:wells-taylor-error"></a>
Choose $\xi$ sufficiently small compared to $c$ and the fixed analytic radius in [lem:wells-analytic](#lem:wells-analytic), putting the hat supports inside that smaller ball after rescaling. With $L_0$ a sufficiently large fixed multiple of $D$, its Taylor polynomial $p_w$ satisfies $$|\psi_i-p_w|+R|\nabla\psi_i-\nabla p_w|\le e^{-20D}
 \quad\text{on }\operatorname{supp}b_w.
 \label{eq:wells-taylor-error}$$ Increasing the fixed multiple absorbs the polynomial factor in the differentiated remainder. The Taylor coefficients establish existence of an approximant; the algorithm will not compute them.

To remove tiny neighborhoods of the poles and the exterior, let $a_{\mathrm{out}}$ equal one up to radius $.66D$, decrease linearly to zero by $.72D$, and vanish thereafter. Let $a_{\mathrm{in}}$ vanish up to $\delta$, increase linearly to one by $2\delta$, and equal one thereafter. Sample $$a_{\mathrm{out}}(|z|)\,
 a_{\mathrm{in}}(|z|)\,
 a_{\mathrm{in}}(|z-\rho_i e_z|)$$ at all grid nodes to get weights $c_w\in[0,1]$, and put $b=\sum_w c_wb_w$. Nonzero weights occur only at retained nodes. The function $b$ equals one away from $O(\delta)$-balls about the poles and away from $|z|>.65D$: a node within $2\delta$ of a pole has adjacent lengths $O(\xi\delta)$, whereas all intervals have length at most $\xi$. These facts localize the inner and outer transitions, respectively.

<a id="eq:wells-cutoff-error"></a>
Each first derivative of a tensor-linear interpolant is a convex combination of edge secants in that coordinate. Consequently $|\nabla b|\le C/\delta$ on the inner transitions and $|\nabla b|\le C/D$ on the outer transition, irrespective of aspect ratios. The bounded sup norm of $\psi_i$ makes the inner cutoff-gradient cost $C\delta^{1/2}$. Also, by its $H^2$ bound, $$\|\nabla\psi_i\|_{L^2(B_{C\delta}(a))}
 \le C\delta\|\nabla\psi_i\|_6\le C\delta.$$ The exterior cost follows from <a href="#eq:wells-h1-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-h1-tail">[eq:wells-h1-tail]</a>. Thus $$\|\psi_i-b\psi_i\|_{H^1}
 \le C\bigl(\sqrt\delta+e^{-.61D}\bigr).
 \label{eq:wells-cutoff-error}$$ The cutoff weights, like the Taylor coefficients, are only existence coefficients in the explicitly generated space.

Set $v=\sum_w c_wb_wp_w\in\mathcal V_i$. At almost every point at most eight hats are nonzero. Their derivatives cost at most $C/\delta$, by the minimum interval length, and $R^{-1}\le C/\delta$. On a region of volume $O(D^3)$, <a href="#eq:wells-taylor-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-taylor-error">[eq:wells-taylor-error]</a> gives $$\|b\psi_i-v\|_{H^1}\le C D^{3/2}\delta^{-1}e^{-20D}.$$ Since $\sqrt\delta=e^{-4(\log2)D}$ and $\delta^{-1}e^{-20D}=e^{-(20-8\log2)D}$, this and <a href="#eq:wells-cutoff-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-cutoff-error">[eq:wells-cutoff-error]</a> prove <a href="#eq:wells-ritz-h1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-ritz-h1">[eq:wells-ritz-h1]</a> for large $D$.

Finally, the form of $h_i-\lambda_i$ is bounded in absolute value by a constant times the $H^1$ norm squared. For $e=v-\psi_i$, the eigen-equation cancels the cross terms, giving $$\frac{\langle v,h_iv\rangle}{\|v\|_2^2}-\lambda_i
 =\frac{\langle e,(h_i-\lambda_i)e\rangle}{\|v\|_2^2}
 \le C\|e\|_{H^1}^2.$$ Here $\|v\|_2$ is bounded away from zero. The variational principle supplies the opposite inequality for the lowest Ritz value, proving <a href="#eq:wells-ritz-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-ritz-bias">[eq:wells-ritz-bias]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Certified polynomial-bit eigenvalue evaluation

A small Ritz space by itself does not establish bit complexity. We next bound the conditioning loss and give certified matrix entries and eigenvalue enclosures. All arithmetic below is classical.

<a id="stmt-prop-ritz-oracle"></a>
<div id="prop:ritz-oracle" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Uniform single-well eigenvalue oracle). *Suppose the centers and the parameters in $[1,4]^m$ are rational, and all their numerators and denominators, as well as those of $q,q'$, have at most $B$ bits. Under the hypotheses of this section, a deterministic algorithm returns rational numbers $\ell_i\le\lambda_i\le u_i$ with <a id="eq:wells-oracle-precision"></a>$$u_i-\ell_i\le\varepsilon_D,\qquad
 \varepsilon_D=2^{-\lceil8D/5\rceil}<e^{-1.1D}.
 \label{eq:wells-oracle-precision}$$ Its bit complexity is polynomial in $D,m,B$. The bound is uniform over every rational sample in $[1,4]^m$; no spectral gap of a Ritz matrix is assumed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-ritz-oracle"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ritz-oracle</summary>

*Proof.* We divide the construction into exact polynomial arithmetic, certified Coulomb integration, and rational eigenvalue enclosure.

*Exact matrices and a mass lower bound.* Construct the basis in [lem:wells-ritz-approximation](#lem:wells-ritz-approximation). All grid decisions are rational comparisons: one-dimensional distances in <a href="#eq:wells-grid-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-grid-rule">[eq:wells-grid-rule]</a> are rational, and node membership in <a href="#eq:wells-retained-nodes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-retained-nodes">[eq:wells-retained-nodes]</a> is checked by comparing squared distances. Grid depth is $O(D+\log D)$. Endpoints, hat coefficients, and coefficients of the degree-$O(D)$ monomials have polynomial bit length. For instance, raising a rational coordinate of $b$ bits to degree $L$ costs at most $O(Lb)$ bits, before polynomial sums; here $b=O(B+D+\log D)$ and $L=O(D)$. Only polynomially many coefficients and rational operations occur in any polynomial expansion or box integral. Summing rational terms by a product of their denominators gives a conservative polynomial bit bound even without exploiting common denominators.

Let $f_1,\ldots,f_{r_0}$ be this possibly redundant basis, $r_0=O(D^6)$. The mass and kinetic matrices <a id="eq:wells-exact-matrices"></a>$$M_{ab}=\int f_af_b\,\mathrm dy,\qquad
 T_{ab}=\frac12\int\nabla f_a\cdot\nabla f_b\,\mathrm dy
 \label{eq:wells-exact-matrices}$$ are exact rationals obtained by box integration. A product of two hats has only a fixed number of box pieces; alternatively, summing over the entire polynomial-size grid also suffices. Remove redundant functions by exact rational Gram-matrix elimination, retaining an independent subset. This gives a positive definite matrix $M$ of dimension $r\le r_0$, with corresponding kinetic matrix $T$. The subset is nonempty by the approximation property.

Let $B_0$ be the largest bit length of a numerator or positive denominator of the entries of this reduced $M$, enlarged to at least one. It is computed from the exact entries and is polynomial in $D,m,B$. If $Q$ is the product of all entry denominators, then $Q\le2^{r^2B_0}$ and $QM$ is an integer positive definite matrix. Therefore $\det(QM)\ge1$, while $\lambda_{\max}(M)\le r2^{B_0}$. The explicitly computable number <a id="eq:wells-mass-lower"></a>$$\mu=2^{-K_M},\qquad
 K_M=r^3B_0+(r-1)\bigl(B_0+\lceil\log_2r\rceil\bigr)
 \label{eq:wells-mass-lower}$$ satisfies $\lambda_{\min}(M)\ge\mu$. This intentionally coarse determinant bound is enough: $K_M$ is polynomial. Exact rank selection is itself polynomial-bit arithmetic. After clearing denominators, fraction-free elimination has entries given by minors of an integer matrix. Hadamard’s determinant bound bounds their bit lengths by a polynomial in its dimension and entry bit length. Equivalently, ordinary rational elimination with fractions reduced at each step has the same polynomial bound through the bordered-minor formulas.

<a id="eq:wells-coulomb-series"></a>
*Certified Coulomb entries.* The preceding determinant bound shows how many extra bits compensate for a poorly conditioned basis. It remains to compute each potential entry to that absolute accuracy. The graded supports avoid every pole, which permits a uniformly convergent kernel expansion on each box. On the supports of the basis functions every other cap equals one, so every potential entry is a finite sum of pure Coulomb integrals against polynomials on rational boxes. For each such box $Q_0$, each pole $a$, and its midpoint $c_0$, put $$s=|c_0-a|^2,\qquad
 z(y)=\frac{2(c_0-a)\cdot(y-c_0)+|y-c_0|^2}{s}.$$ Every contributing box is in the support of a retained hat. By <a href="#eq:wells-patch-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-patch-radius">[eq:wells-patch-radius]</a>, its diameter is a fixed small fraction of its distance from either own pole. Remote poles are farther still. Decreasing the fixed $\xi$ if necessary gives $|z(y)|\le1/2$ throughout every such box. Thus $$\frac1{|y-a|}
 =s^{-1/2}\sum_{j=0}^J\binom{-1/2}{j}z(y)^j
       +\mathcal R_J(y),\qquad
 |\mathcal R_J(y)|\le s^{-1/2}2^{-J}.
 \label{eq:wells-coulomb-series}$$ The coefficient bound $\left|\binom{-1/2}{j}\right|
 =4^{-j}\binom{2j}{j}\le1$ proves this geometric remainder bound by summing the tail.

Here $s$ and all coefficients of $z$ are exact rationals. Both $\log_2^+(s^{-1/2})$ and the bit lengths of these rationals are polynomial in $D,m,B$; indeed the own-pole distances on supports are bounded below by a fixed multiple of $\delta$. The positive scalar $s^{-1/2}$ can be approximated to any requested polynomial number of bits by rational bisection and exact squaring.

We spell out an absolute-error certificate, so cancellation among polynomial terms causes no difficulty. If $P$ is the polynomial multiplying a kernel on $Q_0$, a rational coefficient-and-volume bound gives $$A_{Q_0}\ge\int_{Q_0}|P(y)|\,\mathrm dy.$$ One may take the volume times the sum of absolute coefficients, each multiplied by the appropriate coordinate bound to its degree. Its bit length is polynomial. Include the charge in this bound. If $s^{-1/2}\le2^b$, the truncation error is at most $A_{Q_0}2^{b-J}$. An approximation to $s^{-1/2}$ of absolute error $2^{-q_0}$ adds at most $2A_{Q_0}2^{-q_0}$, since the absolute sum in <a href="#eq:wells-coulomb-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-coulomb-series">[eq:wells-coulomb-series]</a> is at most two. For $T_0$ box–pole summands, taking $$\begin{aligned}
 J&\ge p+b+\lceil\log_2^+ A_{Q_0}\rceil
                         +\lceil\log_2 T_0\rceil+3,\\
 q_0&\ge p+\lceil\log_2^+ A_{Q_0}\rceil
                         +\lceil\log_2 T_0\rceil+4
\end{aligned}$$ makes their total error smaller than $2^{-p}$. Use maxima over the summands to choose common orders. Here $\log_2^+t=\max\{0,\log_2t\}$; integer upper bounds can be read directly from rational numerator and denominator bit lengths.

The polynomial $z(y)^j$ has degree $2j$ in three variables. Even after multiplication by the basis polynomials it has only $O((D+J+1)^3)$ possible monomials. Polynomial multiplication, integration, and coefficient bit lengths are polynomial in $D,m,B,J,q_0$. These bounds establish a deterministic entry algorithm with any prescribed polynomial number $p$ of absolute precision bits. No matrix element is computed by integrating across a Coulomb singularity or a cap transition.

<a id="eq:wells-relative-matrix-error"></a>
*Matrix error and exact eigenvalue decisions.* Let $A$ be the exact form matrix of $h_i$ in the independent basis. Compute a symmetric rational matrix $\widetilde A$ by evaluating each potential entry, once per unordered index pair, to error at most $$\tau=\frac{\varepsilon_D\mu}{16r}.$$ The kinetic matrix is exact. Since a symmetric matrix’s operator norm is at most $r$ times its largest absolute entry, $$\|M^{-1/2}(\widetilde A-A)M^{-1/2}\|
 \le\varepsilon_D/16.
 \label{eq:wells-relative-matrix-error}$$ The required entry precision has $O(D+K_M+\log r)$ bits and is therefore polynomial. Let $\widetilde\lambda$ be the minimum of the generalized Rayleigh quotient for $(\widetilde A,M)$. The variational characterization and <a href="#eq:wells-relative-matrix-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-relative-matrix-error">[eq:wells-relative-matrix-error]</a> give $$|\widetilde\lambda-\lambda_i^{\mathrm R}|
 \le\varepsilon_D/16.$$

An initial rational bracket is $[-R_0,R_0]$, where $$R_0=1+\frac{r\max_{a,b}|\widetilde A_{ab}|}{\mu}.$$ Its bit length is polynomial. To bisect it at a rational $z$, test the inertia of $\widetilde A-zM$ exactly. For clarity, an elementary exact inertia algorithm repeatedly uses congruence and Schur complements. A nonzero diagonal entry is a one-dimensional pivot and contributes its sign. If every remaining diagonal vanishes but an off-diagonal entry $a\ne0$ remains, pivot on the block $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&a\\a&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, which contributes one positive and one negative direction. A zero remaining matrix contributes only zero directions. These operations give the full inertia. After denominators are cleared, each Schur entry is a ratio of bordered minors; determinant bounds again give polynomial bit growth. Thus this test is a polynomial-time exact rational calculation, including the case of zero eigenvalues.

Because $M$ is positive definite, $\widetilde A-zM$ is positive semidefinite exactly when $z\le\widetilde\lambda$. Update the lower or upper endpoint accordingly. Polynomially many bisections give a rational enclosure $[\ell,u]$ of $\widetilde\lambda$ of width at most $\varepsilon_D/8$. Arbitrary proximity to a bisection point incurs no further precision requirement and no eigenvalue-gap assumption.

The fixed lower bound on $D$ can be enlarged so that $Ce^{-1.16D}\le\varepsilon_D/8$; this is possible since $(8/5)\log2<1.16$. Combining the one-sided Ritz bias and the two-sided matrix error gives the certified interval <a id="eq:wells-certified-enclosure"></a>$$\lambda_i\in
 \left[\ell-\frac{\varepsilon_D}{16}
              -\frac{\varepsilon_D}{8},
       u+\frac{\varepsilon_D}{16}\right].
 \label{eq:wells-certified-enclosure}$$ Its width is at most $3\varepsilon_D/8$. Notice that the Ritz bias is subtracted from the lower endpoint; a Ritz upper bound alone would not suffice for tuning. This proves the proposition.

All dimensions, operation counts, and working bit lengths are polynomial in $D,m,B$. The mesh and the independent subset may change at different rational samples, but every such sample obeys the same estimates. Constants controlling the polynomial degree, the cutoff size, and the minimum admissible $D$ are fixed once from the uniform inequalities above. They are not input-dependent advice or a search for an unknown spectral gap. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Simultaneous rational tuning

The energy oracle now supplies certified intervals, while [lem:wells-secants](#lem:wells-secants) supplies the monotonicity and weak coupling of the tuning parameters. Combining these facts gives a rational algorithm: each coordinate is bracketed separately, and simultaneous updates contract. The stopping rule below uses an interval containing the target energy; it never asks for the exact sign of an unknown eigenvalue difference.

<a id="stmt-prop-tuning"></a>
<div id="prop:tuning" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Tuning with fixed rounded charges). *At any fixed rational centers satisfying the hypotheses of this section, and with the integer charges in <a href="#eq:wells-charges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-charges">[eq:wells-charges]</a> already fixed, one can compute rational $\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_m\in[1,4]$ such that <a id="eq:wells-tuned"></a>$$|\lambda_i+1/2|\le e^{-1.01D}\qquad(1\le i\le m).
 \label{eq:wells-tuned}$$ The algorithm is deterministic and polynomial in $D,m$ and the input bit lengths. Every parameter it samples or outputs has $O(D)$ fractional bits.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tuning"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tuning</summary>

*Proof.* For frozen parameters other than $\rho_i$, the endpoint brackets, continuity, and strict secant bound in [lem:wells-secants](#lem:wells-secants) give a unique root of $\lambda_i=-1/2$ in $[1,4]$. Let $T_i(\rho)$ be that root and let $T=(T_1,\ldots,T_m)$, using the old other coordinates in every update. Combining <a href="#eq:wells-own-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-own-slope">[eq:wells-own-slope]</a> and <a href="#eq:wells-other-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-other-slope">[eq:wells-other-slope]</a> gives <a id="eq:wells-contraction"></a>$$\|T(\rho)-T(\widetilde\rho)\|_\infty
 \le\theta\|\rho-\widetilde\rho\|_\infty,\qquad
 \theta=Cm/D^2\le1/2.
 \label{eq:wells-contraction}$$ Thus $T$ has a unique fixed point $\rho^*$ in the cube. This also follows directly by iterating the map: its successive differences are geometrically summable, and the limit is fixed by continuity.

We implement each coordinate update with a certified scalar bisection. Start with $[1,4]$. At its midpoint invoke [prop:ritz-oracle](#prop:ritz-oracle). If the whole resulting eigenvalue interval lies below $-1/2$, retain the upper half of the parameter interval; if it lies above $-1/2$, retain the lower half. If it contains $-1/2$, stop and return that sample. In this last case the true energy differs from $-1/2$ by at most $\varepsilon_D$, so <a href="#eq:wells-own-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wells-own-slope">[eq:wells-own-slope]</a> puts the sample within $C\varepsilon_D/q'$ of the exact root. Otherwise, after $J=\lceil4D\rceil$ bisections return the midpoint of the remaining parameter interval. All sign decisions were certified, so that interval still contains the root and has length $3\,2^{-J}$. Since $q'\le1$ and $2^{-4D}\ll\varepsilon_D$, the same update-error bound holds: <a id="eq:wells-update-error"></a>$$\|\widehat T(\rho)-T(\rho)\|_\infty
 \le C\varepsilon_D/q'.
 \label{eq:wells-update-error}$$ This comparison-or-stop rule never requests an exact sign of an unknown irrational eigenvalue at a putative zero.

Start with all coordinates equal to two and perform $J$ approximate simultaneous updates. If $e_j$ is the sup-norm distance to $\rho^*$, then $$e_{j+1}\le\tfrac12 e_j+C\varepsilon_D/q',
 \qquad
 e_J\le3\,2^{-J}+2C\varepsilon_D/q'.$$ The own-coordinate upper slope and the other-coordinate variation bound together give $$|\lambda_i(\rho)-\lambda_i(\rho^*)|
 \le Cq'\|\rho-\rho^*\|_\infty.$$ Consequently the output has $$|\lambda_i+1/2|
 \le Cq'2^{-J}+C\varepsilon_D
 \le e^{-1.01D}$$ for large enough $D$, since $(8/5)\log2>1.1>1.01$.

Each scalar bisection restarts from the rational interval $[1,4]$ and makes at most $O(D)$ midpoint operations. Its samples, including early-return samples, therefore have $O(D)$ fractional bits independently of the number of simultaneous iterations. There are $O(mD^2)$ oracle calls. Their data bit lengths consist of the fixed-center and rounded-charge bit lengths together with these $O(D)$ parameter bits, so [prop:ritz-oracle](#prop:ritz-oracle) proves the total polynomial-time bound. In particular the charge-rounding error is part of every evaluated operator and is compensated by tuning; it is not introduced as an uncontrolled perturbation afterward. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hopping"></a>
### Relative tails and computable hopping calibration

The absolute orbital estimate of Proposition <a href="#prop:isolated-well" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:isolated-well">8</a> does not determine an overlap of order $e^{-D}$. This section proves a relative tail estimate, identifies positive integrals that determine the hoppings, and shows how to calibrate those integrals before tuning the single-well energies. All constants below are independent of the site layout, $D$, the displacements, and the parameters $\rho_i\in[1,4]$. The cap profile $\chi$ is fixed throughout.

We use the positions and operators from Section <a href="#sec:wells" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:wells">3</a>. In particular, $$X_i=Dp_i+s_i,\qquad \max_i|s_i|\le S,
 \qquad |X_i-X_j|\ge D-2S\quad(i\ne j).$$ We take $D$ sufficiently large and, for the estimates below, may assume $m+S\le D^{1/4}$. Where a relative error is expanded, its displayed bound is assumed smaller than a fixed positive constant. The final parameter choice in Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">6</a> satisfies these conditions and makes every such error as small as required.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Relative comparison of single-well tails

For $r_i=|y-X_i|$ define the ray primitive <a id="eq:ray-primitive"></a>$$F_i(y)=r_i\int_0^1 W_i\bigl(X_i+t(y-X_i)\bigr)\,\,\mathrm dt,
 \qquad F_i(X_i)=0.
 \label{eq:ray-primitive}$$ The integral follows the entire segment from $X_i$ to $y$; it is not a local approximation to the field at either endpoint. Write <a id="eq:relative-tail-error"></a>$$\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}
   =\frac{m\log D+m^2}{D}.
 \label{eq:relative-tail-error}$$

<a id="stmt-prop-relative-tail"></a>
<div id="prop:relative-tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:relative-tail"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Uniform relative tail). *Suppose the actual rounded charges and the parameters $\rho_i$ obey the conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:tuning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuning">14</a>, in particular $|\lambda_i+1/2|\le e^{-1.01D}$. Uniformly for $r_i\le2D$, $$\psi_i(y)=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-r_i+F_i(y)}
       \bigl(1+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})\bigr).
 \label{eq:relative-tail}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-relative-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-relative-tail</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a site, suppress its subscript, and write $r=|y-X|$ and $\omega=(y-X)/r$ when $r>0$. The capped-field estimates in Proposition <a href="#prop:isolated-well" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:isolated-well">8</a> give <a id="eq:hopping-field-derivatives"></a>$$\|W\|_\infty\le \frac{Cm}{D},\qquad
 \|\nabla W\|_\infty\le \frac{Cm}{D^2},\qquad
 \|D^2W\|_\infty\le \frac{Cm}{D^3}.
 \label{eq:hopping-field-derivatives}$$ Set $A(y)=\int_0^1W(X+t(y-X))\,\,\mathrm dt$, so $F=rA$. For $0<r\le3D$, differentiation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \nabla F
  &=\omega A+r\int_0^1t\nabla W(X+t(y-X))\,\,\mathrm dt,\\
 \Delta F
  &=\frac{2A}{r}
    +2\omega\cdot\int_0^1t\nabla W(X+t(y-X))\,\,\mathrm dt\\
  &\quad+r\int_0^1t^2\Delta W(X+t(y-X))\,\,\mathrm dt.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently, <a id="eq:ray-derivative-bounds"></a>$$|\nabla F|\le \frac{Cm}{D},\qquad
 |\Delta F|\le C\left(\frac{m}{Dr}+\frac{m}{D^2}\right).
 \label{eq:ray-derivative-bounds}$$ Also $F(r,\omega)=\int_0^rW(X+t\omega)\,\,\mathrm dt$, and hence $\partial_rF=W(y)$ exactly.

<a id="eq:relative-tail-residual-bound"></a>
Let $f=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-r+F}$. A direct calculation on the annulus $10\le r\le3D$ gives <a id="eq:relative-tail-residual"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{(h_i-\lambda_i)f}{f}
  &=-(\lambda_i+1/2)+\frac1r-V_i
       -\frac12\bigl(\Delta F+|\nabla F|^2\bigr),
       \label{eq:relative-tail-residual}\\
 \left|\frac{(h_i-\lambda_i)f}{f}\right|
  &\le C\left(\frac{m}{Dr}+\frac{m^2}{D^2}\right).
       \label{eq:relative-tail-residual-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ In the first equality the term $\partial_rF$ cancels $W_i$. For the second, note that $\rho_i\le4$ and $r\ge10$, so $$\left|\frac1r-V_i(y)\right|
 \le \frac{|1-q|}{r}+\frac{q'}{r-4}
 \le \frac{Cm}{Dr}.$$ The charge-rounding errors and $e^{-1.01D}$ are absorbed by the right-hand side of <a href="#eq:relative-tail-residual-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-tail-residual-bound">[eq:relative-tail-residual-bound]</a>.

Choose a sufficiently large fixed constant $A_0$ and put <a id="eq:tail-barrier-correction"></a>$$g(r)=A_0\left[
     \frac{m}{D}\bigl(1+\log(r/10)\bigr)
     +\frac{m^2r}{D^2}\right].
 \label{eq:tail-barrier-correction}$$ Then $$g'=A_0\left(\frac{m}{Dr}+\frac{m^2}{D^2}\right),\qquad
 \Delta g=A_0\left(\frac{m}{Dr^2}+\frac{2m^2}{D^2r}\right),
 \qquad 0\le\Delta g\le\frac{2g'}r.$$ Replacing $f$ by $fe^{\pm g}$ adds to its operator quotient the term <a id="eq:barrier-signs"></a>$$\pm(1-\partial_rF)g'
       \mp\frac12\Delta g-\frac12(g')^2.
 \label{eq:barrier-signs}$$ Since $r\ge10$, $\partial_rF=O(m/D)$, and $g'$ is uniformly small for sufficiently large $D$, the added term is at least $c g'$ for the plus sign and at most $-c g'$ for the minus sign. Increasing $A_0$ dominates <a href="#eq:relative-tail-residual-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-tail-residual-bound">[eq:relative-tail-residual-bound]</a>. Thus $fe^g$ is a supersolution and $fe^{-g}$ a subsolution.

At $r=10$, Proposition <a href="#prop:isolated-well" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:isolated-well">8</a> and Sobolev embedding give $\psi_i=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-10}+O(m/D)$, while $F=O(m/D)$. The reference value $e^{-10}$ is a fixed positive number. A further fixed increase of $A_0$ therefore ensures $fe^{-g}\le\psi_i\le fe^g$ on the inner boundary. On the outer boundary $r=3D$, Lemma <a href="#lem:well-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:well-tail">10</a> gives $$|\psi_i|\le C e^{-0.99(3-0.01)D}=C e^{-2.9601D}.$$ Moreover $0\le F\le Cm$ there. With $\kappa=e^{-2.5D}$, the functions $$f_+=fe^g+\kappa,\qquad f_-=fe^{-g}-\kappa$$ satisfy $f_-\le\psi_i\le f_+$ on both boundary spheres for large $D$. On the annulus the zero-order coefficient obeys $$-V_i-W_i-\lambda_i
 \ge \frac12-\frac1{10}-O(m/D)\ge\frac14.$$ Adding the positive constant $\kappa$ preserves the supersolution inequality, and subtracting it preserves the subsolution inequality. The maximum principle therefore yields the same inequalities throughout the annulus. For completeness, its weak form here follows by testing a supersolution-minus-solution with its nonnegative negative part: the resulting identity is the negative of a sum of a gradient norm and a strictly positive weighted $L^2$ norm, so the negative part vanishes. The same argument applies to a solution-minus-subsolution.

On $10\le r\le2D$, one has $g(r)=O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})$ and, since $F\ge0$, $$\frac{\kappa}{f}\le \sqrt\pi\,e^{-D/2}.$$ The comparison inequalities imply <a href="#eq:relative-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-tail">[eq:relative-tail]</a> on this region. On $r\le10$, the reference exponential is bounded below by a fixed positive constant, $F=O(m/D)$, and the uniform absolute orbital estimate gives the same relative conclusion directly. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Surrogate fields and positive hopping integrals

We first identify the attraction integral to be calibrated. Write $V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}=(1-\chi_k)V_k$ for each site $k$, and for an ordered pair of distinct sites $(i,j)$ define <a id="eq:oriented-hole-integral"></a>$$B_{ij}=\langle\psi_j,V_j^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i\rangle.
 \label{eq:oriented-hole-integral}$$ The identity $$h=h_i-\sum_{k\ne i}V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}$$ shows that the hole at $j$ contributes $-B_{ij}$ to the matrix element $\langle\psi_j,(h+1/2)\psi_i\rangle$ in the nonorthogonal orbitals. Section <a href="#sec:continuum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuum">5</a> will bound its remaining terms and the change under orthonormalization.

The relative tail formula still contains the secondary parameters, which have not yet been computed when the primary centers must be placed. We replace its capped field by an explicit one depending only on the primary centers. The replacement error is relative and uniform in the entire tuning cube; this is what makes the construction order possible.

Define <a id="eq:surrogate-field"></a>$$W_i^*(y)=\sum_{k\ne i}\frac{\chi(|y-X_k|/D)}{|y-X_k|},\qquad
 F_i^*(y)=r_i\int_0^1W_i^*(X_i+t(y-X_i))\,\,\mathrm dt.
 \label{eq:surrogate-field}$$ Each summand is extended by zero through its cutoff hole. In particular the apparent quotient at $y=X_k$ is identically zero in a neighborhood of that point.

<a id="stmt-lem-surrogate"></a>
<div id="lem:surrogate" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:capped-kernel-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:surrogate-error"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Uniform capped kernels and surrogate replacement). *The zero-extended kernels $$G_D(z)=\frac{\chi(|z|/D)}{|z|},\qquad
 J_{D,\rho}(z)=\frac{\chi(|z|/D)}{|z-\rho e_z|}$$ are globally $C^2$, uniformly for $1\le\rho\le4$. For every multi-index $\alpha$ with $|\alpha|\le2$, $$\|\partial^\alpha G_D\|_\infty+
 \|\partial^\alpha J_{D,\rho}\|_\infty
 \le C D^{-1-|\alpha|}.
 \label{eq:capped-kernel-bounds}$$ Uniformly over all allowed secondary positions and rounded charges, $$\|W_i-W_i^*\|_\infty\le\frac{Cm^2}{D^2},\qquad
 |F_i(y)-F_i^*(y)|\le\frac{Cm^2}{D}\quad(r_i\le3D).
 \label{eq:surrogate-error}$$ Consequently, for tuned wells the relative formula <a href="#eq:relative-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-tail">[eq:relative-tail]</a> holds with $F_i^*$ in place of $F_i$ and the same error bound $O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-surrogate"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-surrogate</summary>

*Proof.* For large $D$, both poles are strictly inside $|z|<D/16$, where the kernels vanish. On the nonzero part of either kernel, $|z|\ge D/16$ and $|z-\rho e_z|\ge |z|/2$. Rescale by $z=D\zeta$. Both kernels are $D^{-1}$ times a function whose derivatives through order two are uniformly bounded: the shifted pole in the second rescaled kernel has position $(\rho/D)e_z$, a fixed positive distance from the support of $\chi$. The $C^2$ matching of $\chi$ at its transition endpoints makes these global bounds, including at the hole boundaries. This proves <a href="#eq:capped-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capped-kernel-bounds">[eq:capped-kernel-bounds]</a>.

Since $|q-1|+q'\le Cm/D$ after rounding, $$W_i-W_i^*=
  \sum_{k\ne i}\bigl[(q-1)G_D(y-X_k)
                  +q'J_{D,\rho_k}(y-X_k)\bigr]$$ has sup norm $O(m^2/D^2)$. Integration over a ray of length at most $3D$ proves the second bound in <a href="#eq:surrogate-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surrogate-error">[eq:surrogate-error]</a>. Exponentiating this bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-tail">15</a> proves the final assertion. None of the kernel estimates require tuning; they hold uniformly on the whole parameter cube. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:hopping-log-integrand"></a>
For an oriented contact $(i,j)$, replacing the two orbital factors in $B_{ij}$ by their relative approximations and the local nuclear potential by the unit primary kernel leads to the explicit positive integral <a id="eq:positive-hopping-integral"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 T_{ij}(X)
  &=\frac1\pi\int_{|v|\le D/8}
       \frac{1-\chi(|v|/D)}{|v|}\,e^{L_X(v)}\,\,\mathrm d^3v,
       \label{eq:positive-hopping-integral}\\
 L_X(v)
  &=-|X_j+v-X_i|-|v|
     +F_i^*(X_j+v)+F_j^*(X_j+v).
       \label{eq:hopping-log-integrand}
\end{aligned}$$ The fields in this definition use all centers in $X$. The integrable singularity $|v|^{-1}$ is part of the nonnegative integration weight, not part of the exponential. The next lemma justifies these replacements.

<a id="stmt-lem-positive-hopping"></a>
<div id="lem:positive-hopping" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hole-surrogate-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Comparison with the uncapped attraction). *For tuned wells and every contact, $$B_{ij}=T_{ij}(X)
       \bigl(1+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})\bigr).
 \label{eq:hole-surrogate-comparison}$$ The constants are uniform in $\rho_j\in[1,4]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-positive-hopping"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-hopping</summary>

*Proof.* On the support of $V_j^{\mathrm{hole}}$, write $y=X_j+v$. Then $|v|\le D/8$ and $|y-X_i|\le D+2S+D/8<2D$. Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-tail">15</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:surrogate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:surrogate">16</a> apply to both wave functions. Their product is $\pi^{-1}e^{L_X(v)}$ times a uniform $1+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})$ factor. The primary kernel has coefficient $q=1+O(m/D)$, so it remains to compare the secondary kernel with the primary one in the resulting positive integral.

For $|v|>10$, $$\frac{|v|}{|v-\rho_j e_z|}
   \le\frac{|v|}{|v|-4}\le\frac53.$$ For $|v|\le10$, the hole weight is one when $D$ is large. The ray derivative estimates, also valid for $F^*$, show that $L_X$ is Lipschitz on this ball with a fixed constant. This statement requires no differentiability of $|v|$ at zero; it follows either by integrating the bounded derivatives away from zero or directly from the ray formula. Thus $e^{L_X(v)}$ is within fixed factors of $e^{L_X(0)}$ throughout the ball. Moreover, $$\int_{|v|\le10}\frac{\,\mathrm d^3v}{|v-\rho_j e_z|}
  \le\int_{|w|\le14}\frac{\,\mathrm d^3w}{|w|}<\infty,
 \qquad
 \int_{|v|\le1}\frac{\,\mathrm d^3v}{|v|}>0.$$ The secondary-kernel integral on the inner ball is therefore at most a fixed multiple of the primary-kernel integral. Combining the two regions and multiplying by $q'=O(m/D)$ gives a relative $O(m/D)$ correction. Positivity allows the uniform relative wave-function errors to be taken outside both integrals. These errors are included in <a href="#eq:hole-surrogate-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hole-surrogate-comparison">[eq:hole-surrogate-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-reference-hopping"></a>
<div id="lem:reference-hopping" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:reference-hopping-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Size of the reference integrals). *Let $X^0=(Dp_i)_i$. For every contact, $$c e^{-D}\le T_{ij}(X^0)\le C D^2 e^{-D+Cm}.
 \label{eq:reference-hopping-bounds}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reference-hopping"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reference-hopping</summary>

*Proof.* Put $u=u_{ij}=p_j-p_i$, so $|u|=1$. The triangle inequality gives $|Du+v|+|v|\ge D$. Each ray correction in the integral is nonnegative and at most $Cm$, by <a href="#eq:capped-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capped-kernel-bounds">[eq:capped-kernel-bounds]</a> and its ray length. The integral of $|v|^{-1}$ over the ball of radius $D/8$ is $O(D^2)$, proving the upper bound. On $|v|\le1$ the hole weight is one, $|Du+v|+|v|\le D+2$, and the corrections are nonnegative. Integration over this fixed ball gives the lower bound; for example $2e^{-D-2}$ is a valid lower bound for large $D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Sensitivity to displacement

<a id="stmt-lem-hopping-displacement"></a>
<div id="lem:hopping-displacement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Uniform displacement law). *Let $X_i=Dp_i+s_i$ with $|s_i|\le S$. There is a constant $c_1>0$ such that, uniformly on all contacts, <a id="eq:hopping-displacement-law"></a><a id="eq:hopping-displacement-error"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{T_{ij}(X)}{T_{ij}(X^0)}
  &=e^{-u_{ij}\cdot(s_j-s_i)}
     \bigl(1+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{disp}})\bigr),
     \label{eq:hopping-displacement-law}\\
 \varepsilon_{\mathrm{disp}}
  &=\frac{mS}{D}+\frac{S^2}{D}
      +S D^{-1/4}+e^{-c_1\sqrt D}.
     \label{eq:hopping-displacement-error}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular the coefficient of the projected displacement in the leading exponent is one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hopping-displacement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hopping-displacement</summary>

<a id="eq:ray-displacement"></a>
*Proof.* Compare the two integrals at the same $v$. In the ray defining $F_i^*(X_j+v)$, the reference path point $Dp_i+t(Dp_j+v-Dp_i)$ moves by $(1-t)s_i+ts_j$, of norm at most $S$. Each field center moves by at most $S$ as well. The globally Lipschitz bound in <a href="#eq:capped-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capped-kernel-bounds">[eq:capped-kernel-bounds]</a> therefore changes the field along the path by at most $CmS/D^2$. The ray has length $O(D)$ and its length changes by at most $2S$. Since the field itself is $O(m/D)$, $$|F_i^*(X_j+v;X)-F_i^*(Dp_j+v;X^0)|
    \le\frac{CmS}{D}.
 \label{eq:ray-displacement}$$ The same bound holds for the $F_j^*$ term; its ray length $|v|$ does not change. The global nature of the capped-kernel bound is essential here: it also applies when a moving ray crosses a hole or a transition interface.

<a id="eq:distance-displacement"></a>
Write $u=u_{ij}$, $\delta=s_j-s_i$, and $v=zu+w$ with $w\perp u$. Since $|v|\le D/8$, $|Du+v|\ge7D/8$. The Hessian of the norm on the segment from $Du+v$ to $Du+v+\delta$ has norm $O(1/D)$. Its gradient at $Du+v$ differs from $u$ by $O(|w|/D)$. Taylor’s formula gives $$|Du+v+\delta|-|Du+v|
   =u\cdot\delta+O\left(\frac{S|w|}{D}+\frac{S^2}{D}\right).
 \label{eq:distance-displacement}$$ This estimate includes the whole backward segment $v=-tu$, $0\le t\le D/8$: there $Du+v=(D-t)u$ and the gradient is exactly $u$. Thus the segment’s non-negligible longitudinal extent creates neither a different coefficient nor an $O(S)$ transverse error.

<a id="eq:main-region-log-error"></a>
On the region $|w|\le D^{3/4}$, Equations <a href="#eq:ray-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ray-displacement">[eq:ray-displacement]</a> and <a href="#eq:distance-displacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-displacement">[eq:distance-displacement]</a> show that $$L_X(v)-L_{X^0}(v)+u\cdot\delta
   =O\left(\frac{mS}{D}+\frac{S^2}{D}+S D^{-1/4}\right).
 \label{eq:main-region-log-error}$$ The nonnegative integration weight is identical in the two integrals, so exponentiation controls their contributions on this region by the same relative factor.

On the remaining region $|w|>D^{3/4}$, one has <a id="eq:transverse-excess"></a>$$\begin{split}
 |Du+v|+|v|-D
   &\ge z+|v|
    =\frac{|w|^2}{|v|-z}\\
   &\ge\frac{4|w|^2}{D}
    \ge4\sqrt D.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:transverse-excess}$$ The identity is used only for $w\ne0$, and the denominator is at most $2|v|\le D/4$. The field corrections are bounded by $Cm$, without any angular constancy assumption. Displacement changes a distance by at most $2S$, and $|u\cdot\delta|\le2S$. It follows that the omitted reference and displaced contributions, after division by $e^{-D}$ and $e^{-D-u\cdot\delta}$ respectively, are at most <a id="eq:transverse-integral-error"></a>$$C D^2\exp\bigl[-c\sqrt D+C(m+S)\bigr].
 \label{eq:transverse-integral-error}$$ By <a href="#eq:reference-hopping-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-hopping-bounds">[eq:reference-hopping-bounds]</a>, these are also bounds on their contributions relative to the reference integral, with the appropriate displacement factor. The assumption $m+S\le D^{1/4}$ and sufficiently large $D$ absorb both the possibly large anisotropic factor $e^{Cm}$ and the polynomial volume factor into $e^{-c_1\sqrt D}$. Combining this with <a href="#eq:main-region-log-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-region-log-error">[eq:main-region-log-error]</a>, and using smallness of its right-hand side, proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Deterministic relative quadrature

We have reduced hopping calibration to positive integrals whose size is exponentially small. Direct absolute quadrature at that scale would obscure the complexity bound. Instead we control the oscillation of the logarithm of the exponential factor, while integrating its nonnegative radial weight exactly. Positivity then converts local errors into a relative error for the whole integral.

<a id="stmt-lem-hopping-quadrature"></a>
<div id="lem:hopping-quadrature" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:reference-quadrature-accuracy"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Computing the positive reference integrals). *For $0<\epsilon<1/10$, one can compute a positive rational $\widehat I_{ij}$ satisfying $$\left|\log\frac{\widehat I_{ij}}{e^D T_{ij}(X^0)}\right|
   \le\epsilon
 \label{eq:reference-quadrature-accuracy}$$ by a deterministic algorithm polynomial in $D$, $m$, the bit lengths of the layout data, and $\epsilon^{-1}$. In particular the algorithm is polynomial in the source size when $D$ is a fixed polynomial and $\epsilon$ an inverse polynomial.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hopping-quadrature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hopping-quadrature</summary>

<a id="eq:hopping-log-regularity"></a>
*Proof.* Let $L=L_{X^0}$ in <a href="#eq:hopping-log-integrand" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hopping-log-integrand">[eq:hopping-log-integrand]</a>. On the integration ball, the two distance terms have Lipschitz constants one, and the ray formulas and <a href="#eq:capped-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capped-kernel-bounds">[eq:capped-kernel-bounds]</a> give a Lipschitz constant $Cm/D$ for each correction. Hence $$|L(v)-L(v')|\le C|v-v'|,
 \qquad |L(v)|\le C(D+m).
 \label{eq:hopping-log-regularity}$$ Only the exponential factor has been included in this logarithm. No lower bound on $1-\chi$ or differentiability of $\log(1-\chi)$ is needed.

We describe a mesh with exact positive weights. Parameterize the unit sphere, up to a set of area zero, by $$\omega(\mu,\varphi)
   =\bigl(\sqrt{1-\mu^2}\cos\varphi,
           \sqrt{1-\mu^2}\sin\varphi,\mu\bigr).$$ Here $-1\le\mu\le1$, $0\le\varphi\le2\pi$, and $\,\mathrm d\omega=\,\mathrm d\mu\,\,\mathrm d\varphi$. Use $M$ equal intervals in $\mu$, $N$ equal intervals in $\varphi$, and a rational radial partition with mesh at most $h$. Insert all the finitely many breakpoints of $\chi(r/D)$ into that partition. On a radial interval $I_a$, the weight $$w_a=\int_{I_a}(1-\chi(r/D))r\,\,\mathrm dr$$ is a nonnegative exact rational, computable by polynomial integration. Discard intervals with zero weight. The normalized angular weight of every angular rectangle is exactly <a id="eq:exact-angular-weight"></a>$$\frac1\pi\,\frac2M\,\frac{2\pi}{N}=\frac4{MN}.
 \label{eq:exact-angular-weight}$$ Choose one sample $v_{abc}$ in each resulting spatial cell, for example at the parameter midpoints.

<a id="eq:positive-quadrature-bracket"></a>
The square-root parameterization satisfies $$|\omega(\mu,\varphi)-\omega(\mu',\varphi')|
  \le C|\mu-\mu'|^{1/2}+C|\varphi-\varphi'|.$$ Therefore $M=O((D/h)^2)$ and $N=O(D/h)$, with sufficiently large fixed constants, make every spatial cell have diameter $O(h)$, including cells adjacent to the poles. There are $O((D/h)^4)$ cells, a polynomial number. Their exact weighted sample sum is $$Q=\sum_{a,b,c}\frac{4w_a}{MN}\,e^{L(v_{abc})}.$$ Positivity and <a href="#eq:hopping-log-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hopping-log-regularity">[eq:hopping-log-regularity]</a> imply $$e^{-Ch}Q\le T_{ij}(X^0)\le e^{Ch}Q.
 \label{eq:positive-quadrature-bracket}$$ Thus a fixed multiple of $\epsilon$ is sufficient for $h$. This relative estimate is independent of the size $e^{-D}$ of the integral.

It remains to specify evaluation and bit accuracy. For a ray of length $r\le2D$, the integrand $t\mapsto W_i^*(X_i+t(y-X_i))$ has Lipschitz constant at most $Cmr/D^2$. A midpoint rule with $K$ equal panels therefore approximates its ray primitive with error at most <a id="eq:ray-quadrature-error"></a>$$C\frac{mr^2}{D^2K}\le\frac{Cm}{K}.
 \label{eq:ray-quadrature-error}$$ Taking $K=O(m/\epsilon)$ and evaluating each field sample to absolute error $O(\epsilon/D)$ gives an $O(\epsilon)$ error in each primitive. Each field sample is a sum of $m-1$ globally Lipschitz capped kernels. At small radius the kernel is evaluated as zero, and elsewhere its denominator is bounded below by a fixed multiple of $D$; equivalently one evaluates the fixed continuous function $\chi(a)/a$, extended by zero on $[0,1/16]$, at $a=|z|/D$. Its global Lipschitz bound controls errors even if the approximate radius lies on the other side of a transition breakpoint. No crossing location needs to be found.

The coordinates $Dp_i$, sample coordinates, square roots, and trigonometric values can be computed to inverse-polynomial absolute accuracy with polynomially many bits. The global kernel and ray bounds just proved control the resulting errors. Thus all sample logarithms can be approximated to an arbitrarily specified $O(\epsilon)$ absolute error with polynomial work. Such logarithm errors change the positive sample sum by a factor $e^{O(\epsilon)}$, not by an additive error multiplied by an uncontrolled exponential.

For the remaining arithmetic, compute the scaled sum $e^D Q$, using sample exponentials $e^{D+L(v_{abc})}$. Its exact value is bounded below by a positive constant up to the factor in <a href="#eq:positive-quadrature-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-quadrature-bracket">[eq:positive-quadrature-bracket]</a>, because <a href="#eq:reference-hopping-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-hopping-bounds">[eq:reference-hopping-bounds]</a> gives $e^D T_{ij}(X^0)\ge2e^{-2}$. The sum of its non-exponential weights is $O(D^2)$, and all exponent arguments have magnitude $O(D+m)$. After the logarithms have been approximated as above, each elementary exponential can be rounded to sufficiently small absolute error $O(\epsilon/D^2)$, clipping a negative rounded value to zero; this adds $O(\epsilon)$ to the scaled sum. These last approximations require only $O(D+m+\log(D/\epsilon))$ precision bits, with polynomial working precision. This step does not require exponentially accurate ray quadrature: the earlier ray errors have already been bounded multiplicatively. Standard range reduction and Taylor series compute the square roots, trigonometric values, exponentials, and logarithms to these precisions in polynomial time. The exact rational weights have polynomial bit lengths, and the sum contains polynomially many terms.

Allocate a sufficiently small fixed fraction of $\epsilon$ to each of the spatial, ray, coordinate, and arithmetic errors. The resulting positive rational sum is $\widehat I_{ij}$ and satisfies <a href="#eq:reference-quadrature-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-quadrature-accuracy">[eq:reference-quadrature-accuracy]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Calibration, rounding, and orientation

The oriented attraction integrals are close to a symmetric matrix for an exact reason that is useful independently of their asymptotic formula.

<a id="stmt-lem-hopping-orientation"></a>
<div id="lem:hopping-orientation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hopping-orientation-identity"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Orientation identity). *For distinct sites, $$B_{ij}-B_{ji}
   =(\lambda_i-\lambda_j)\langle\psi_j,\psi_i\rangle.
 \label{eq:hopping-orientation-identity}$$ For tuned wells and large $D$ under the stated separation bounds, <a id="eq:hopping-orientation-error"></a>$$|\langle\psi_j,\psi_i\rangle|\le e^{-0.85D},\qquad
 |B_{ij}-B_{ji}|\le2e^{-1.86D}.
 \label{eq:hopping-orientation-error}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hopping-orientation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hopping-orientation</summary>

*Proof.* The single-well operators have common domain $H^2$, and $$h_i-h_j=V_j^{\mathrm{hole}}-V_i^{\mathrm{hole}}.$$ Pair this identity between the real eigenfunctions $\psi_j$ and $\psi_i$, using self-adjointness of $h_j$, to obtain <a href="#eq:hopping-orientation-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hopping-orientation-identity">[eq:hopping-orientation-identity]</a>. All the pairings are well-defined by the Coulomb domain bounds of Proposition <a href="#prop:isolated-well" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:isolated-well">8</a>. Equivalently, in the two expansions of the full matrix element $\langle\psi_j,(h+1/2)\psi_i\rangle$, contributions from every hole other than $i$ and $j$ cancel identically, because they integrate the same real product against the same multiplication potential.

To record an explicit overlap bound, Lemma <a href="#lem:well-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:well-tail">10</a> gives $$|\psi_i(y)\psi_j(y)|
    \le C e^{0.0198D}e^{-0.99(r_i+r_j)}.$$ Use $r_i+r_j\ge D-2S$ on a factor with exponent $0.90$, and retain $e^{-0.09r_i}$ for integrability. It follows that $$|\langle\psi_j,\psi_i\rangle|
   \le C e^{-0.8802D+1.8S}
          \int_{\mathbb R^3}e^{-0.09|y-X_i|}\,\,\mathrm d^3y
   \le e^{-0.85D}$$ for sufficiently large $D$. Finally $|\lambda_i-\lambda_j|\le2e^{-1.01D}$ proves the second bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:total-calibration-error"></a>
For a tolerance $\tau>0$, define the total calibration error $$\begin{split}
 \mathcal E_{\mathrm{cal}}
  ={}&\frac{m\log D+m^2}{D}
       +\frac{mS}{D}+\frac{S^2}{D}\\
     &+S D^{-1/4}+\tau+e^{-c_1\sqrt D}.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:total-calibration-error}$$

<a id="stmt-prop-calibration"></a>
<div id="prop:calibration" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:calibrated-displacement-size"></a>
<a id="eq:calibrated-contact-error"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Deterministic hopping calibration). *Let the contact layout be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:contact-layout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contact-layout">5</a>, choose one orientation per contact, and let the prescribed rational hoppings satisfy $0<t_{\min}\le t_{ij}\le1$, where $t_{\min}$ is an inverse polynomial in the source size. Let $Z_*$ be an integer with $e^D/2\le Z_*\le2e^D$ and round the nuclear charges as in Section <a href="#sec:wells" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:wells">3</a>. For inverse-polynomial $\tau$, there is a deterministic polynomial-time procedure that computes rational centers $X_i=Dp_i+s_i$ obeying $$S:=\max_i|s_i|
   \le Cm\bigl(m+\log D+\log(1/t_{\min})+1\bigr)+\tau.
 \label{eq:calibrated-displacement-size}$$ After applying Proposition <a href="#prop:tuning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuning">14</a> at these final centers, the resulting orbitals satisfy, on every chosen oriented contact, $$|Z_*B_{ij}-t_{ij}|
      \le C t_{ij}\mathcal E_{\mathrm{cal}}.
 \label{eq:calibrated-contact-error}$$ Here $D$ is taken sufficiently large for the hypotheses above and for $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{cal}}$ to be small. The reverse oriented attraction has an additional error at most $4e^{-0.86D}$ in the same rescaled units. No additional geometric equation for the reverse orientation is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-calibration"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-calibration</summary>

<a id="eq:calibration-increment-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Compute, to additive error at most $\tau/4$, the quantities <a id="eq:calibration-increments"></a>$$d_{ij}=\log\left(\frac{Z_*T_{ij}(X^0)}{t_{ij}}\right),
 \qquad X^0=(Dp_i)_i.
 \label{eq:calibration-increments}$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:hopping-quadrature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopping-quadrature">20</a> supplies the requisite relative integral accuracy. One can evaluate the logarithm as $$\log Z_*-D+\log\bigl(e^D T_{ij}(X^0)\bigr)-\log t_{ij},$$ so all elementary-function calculations have polynomial bit cost. The two-sided bound <a href="#eq:reference-hopping-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-hopping-bounds">[eq:reference-hopping-bounds]</a> yields $$\max_{(i,j)\in\mathcal C}|d_{ij}|
   \le C\bigl(m+\log D+\log(1/t_{\min})+1\bigr).
 \label{eq:calibration-increment-bound}$$

Apply the constructive linear right inverse in Lemma <a href="#lem:displacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:displacement">6</a> to the approximated data. Its operator bound is $Cm$ from the maximum edge datum to the maximum site displacement. Its formulas use rational operations and fixed elements of $\mathbb Q(\sqrt3)$, so they can be evaluated with polynomially many bits. Round the resulting positions $Dp_i+s_i$ to rational positions with Euclidean site error at most $\tau/8$, allocating another such allowance if approximate arithmetic is used in the right inverse. Define the final displacements from these rational positions by $s_i=X_i-Dp_i$. This definition preserves the exact contact vector decomposition even though $s_i$ need not be rational. Since every $u_{ij}$ is a unit vector, the resulting equations satisfy <a id="eq:rounded-displacement-equations"></a>$$|u_{ij}\cdot(s_j-s_i)-d_{ij}|\le\tau.
 \label{eq:rounded-displacement-equations}$$ The right-inverse bound and <a href="#eq:calibration-increment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calibration-increment-bound">[eq:calibration-increment-bound]</a> imply <a href="#eq:calibrated-displacement-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calibrated-displacement-size">[eq:calibrated-displacement-size]</a>. For example, the $\sqrt3$ arithmetic may be done to absolute accuracy $O(\tau/[D+\operatorname{poly}(m)\max(1,\max|d_{ij}|)])$; the required number of bits is polynomial. Noncontacts retain separation at least $\sqrt2D-2S$ by the triangle inequality.

There is no requirement to realize prescribed distances exactly. For a contact with $\delta=s_j-s_i$ the actual distance is $$|Du_{ij}+\delta|
   =D+u_{ij}\cdot\delta+O(S^2/D).$$ Actual coordinates automatically satisfy all vector-cycle identities; the displayed per-edge correction is already included in Lemma <a href="#lem:hopping-displacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopping-displacement">19</a> and is not accumulated through a further nonlinear solve.

Put $e_{ij}=u_{ij}\cdot(s_j-s_i)-d_{ij}$. The key cancellation is the exact identity <a id="eq:exact-calibration-identity"></a>$$Z_*T_{ij}(X^0)e^{-u_{ij}\cdot(s_j-s_i)}
    =t_{ij}e^{-e_{ij}},\qquad |e_{ij}|\le\tau.
 \label{eq:exact-calibration-identity}$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:hopping-displacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopping-displacement">19</a> therefore gives $Z_*T_{ij}(X)=t_{ij}(1+O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{disp}}+\tau))$. After tuning, Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-hopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-hopping">17</a> adds only $O(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})$ relative error. This proves <a href="#eq:calibrated-contact-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calibrated-contact-error">[eq:calibrated-contact-error]</a>. In particular no factor $e^{Cm}$ or $e^{|d_{ij}|}$ multiplies its absolute error, even for the smallest floored hopping. Since $t_{ij}\le1$, the absolute error per contact is at most $C\mathcal E_{\mathrm{cal}}$; summing hopping operators incurs only polynomial factors in the number of sites.

All computations of $X$ used the surrogate fields $W_i^*$. They depend on the primary centers and on $D$, but not on any eigenfunction or on the secondary parameters $\rho_i$. Thus the algorithm first fixes the rounded charges, computes and rounds these centers, and only then invokes Proposition <a href="#prop:tuning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuning">14</a> at the final rational centers. The surrogate-field replacement bounds hold uniformly over the tuning cube, and the relative orbital comparisons hold uniformly over all tuned outputs. Thus tuning at these fixed centers requires no recalibration and introduces no subsequent position-rounding error.

Finally Lemma <a href="#lem:hopping-orientation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hopping-orientation">21</a> and $Z_*\le2e^D$ give $Z_*|B_{ij}-B_{ji}|\le4e^{-0.86D}$. The common target energy $-1/2$ is sufficient; exact equality of the $\lambda_i$ is not required. The full one-body operator is Hermitian, so one may use either orientation for its off-diagonal entry after the negligible other-hole terms are accounted for in Section <a href="#sec:continuum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuum">5</a>. ◻

</details>

If the contact set is empty, the procedure simply rounds $Dp_i$ to rational centers at the stated site accuracy; there are no hopping equations to solve. In general <a href="#eq:calibrated-displacement-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calibrated-displacement-size">[eq:calibrated-displacement-size]</a> is $S\le\operatorname{poly}(n)(1+\log D)$. The bounds needed for the global choice are precisely <a href="#eq:total-calibration-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-calibration-error">[eq:total-calibration-error]</a>, together with $m+S\le D^{1/4}$ and the exponentially small orientation error. All scale choices and the conversion to the final many-electron energy error are made in Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">6</a>.

<a id="sec:continuum"></a>
### Excluding lower-energy continuum states

We now compare the spectral infimum of the full Coulomb operator with the half-filled Hubbard energy. In particular, the comparison below includes a lower bound on every state outside the selected orbital space.

The proof has three distinct outputs. We first identify the one-particle matrix in orthonormal site modes and prove a fixed positive gap on its entire orthogonal complement. We then bound the many-electron coupling to that complement and eliminate it by a quadratic-form square completion. Only after this all-state lower bound is available do we compare the compressed Coulomb interaction with the Hubbard onsite term. Keeping these steps separate prevents a variational upper bound from being used as a NO-instance lower bound.

<a id="eq:continuum-orbital-input"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-tail-input"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-well-gap"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-separations"></a>
We use the rational centers and tuned parameters constructed in Sections <a href="#sec:wells" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:wells">3</a> and <a href="#sec:hopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hopping">4</a>. Increasing the fixed polynomial choice of $D$ if necessary, we may assume throughout this section that $$m+S\le D^{1/4},\qquad D\ge D_0,$$ where the constant $D_0$ can be chosen uniformly. The estimates from Propositions <a href="#prop:isolated-well" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:isolated-well">8</a> and <a href="#prop:tuning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuning">14</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:well-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:well-tail">10</a> that we will use are $$\begin{aligned}
 \|\psi_i-\phi_i\|_{H^2}&\le C m/D,
 &\|\psi_i\|_{H^2}&\le C, \label{eq:continuum-orbital-input}\\
 |\lambda_i+1/2|&\le e^{-1.01D},
 &|\psi_i(y)|&\le C e^{-.99(r_i-.01D)}. \label{eq:continuum-tail-input}
\end{aligned}$$ There is also a constant $g_{\mathrm w}>0$, independent of the construction parameters, such that $$h_i-\lambda_i\ge
 g_{\mathrm w}\bigl(I-|\psi_i\rangle\langle\psi_i|\bigr).
 \label{eq:continuum-well-gap}$$ All operator inequalities involving an unbounded operator are understood as quadratic-form inequalities. By Lemma <a href="#lem:contact-layout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contact-layout">5</a> and the displacement bound, $$|X_i-X_j|\ge D-2S\quad(i\ne j),\qquad
 |X_i-X_j|\ge\sqrt2D-2S\quad(\{i,j\}\notin\mathcal C).
 \label{eq:continuum-separations}$$ The second inequality concerns distinct noncontact sites.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The one-particle matrix

<a id="eq:continuum-calibration-interface"></a>
Recall the hole potentials from <a href="#sec:hopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hopping">4</a>, and write $$A=h+\tfrac12,\qquad
 V_i^{\mathrm{hole}}=(1-\chi_i)V_i,\qquad
 \epsilon_i=\lambda_i+\tfrac12.$$ For the chosen orientation $i\to j$ of a contact, define $$B_{ij}=\langle\psi_j,V_j^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i\rangle,\qquad
 \epsilon_{\mathrm{hop}}
 =\max_{i\to j}\bigl|Z_*B_{ij}-t_{ij}\bigr|.$$ The maximum is zero if there are no contacts. Proposition <a href="#prop:calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:calibration">22</a> gives $$\epsilon_{\mathrm{hop}}\le C\mathcal E_{\mathrm{cal}},
 \label{eq:continuum-calibration-interface}$$ because all the target hoppings are at most one. Let $\mathsf T$ be the real symmetric $m$-by-$m$ matrix with entry $-t_{ij}$ on contacts and zero elsewhere, including its diagonal.

Define $\mathcal U:\mathbb C^m\to L^2(\mathbb R^3)$ by $\mathcal U e_i=\psi_i$, and let $G=\mathcal U^*\mathcal U$ be its Gram matrix. The notation $\mathcal U$ denotes this map; the scalar onsite repulsion remains $U=5/8$.

<a id="stmt-lem-one-body-matrix"></a>
<div id="lem:one-body-matrix" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:continuum-residual-gram"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-one-body-error"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-projected-residual"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Residuals and the orthonormalized matrix). *For the preceding parameters and sufficiently large $D$, $$\|A\psi_i\|_2\le e^{-.75D},\qquad
 \|G-I\|\le e^{-.85D}.
 \label{eq:continuum-residual-gram}$$ In particular $G$ is positive definite. The isometry $\mathcal J_0=\mathcal U G^{-1/2}$ has orthonormal columns $\omega_i=\mathcal J_0e_i$, spanning the same spatial orbital space. Its matrix satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \|Z_*\mathcal J_0^*A\mathcal J_0-\mathsf T\|
 &\le \delta_1, \label{eq:continuum-one-body-error}\\
 \delta_1
 &:=m\epsilon_{\mathrm{hop}}+Z_*e^{-1.01D}
       +CmZ_*e^{-1.1D}+CmZ_*e^{-1.6D}. \notag
\end{aligned}$$ If $P_0=\mathcal J_0\mathcal J_0^*$, then $$\|AP_0\|\le C\sqrt m\,e^{-.75D},
 \qquad
 \|\omega_i-\phi_i\|_{H^1}\le Cm/D.
 \label{eq:continuum-projected-residual}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-one-body-matrix"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-one-body-matrix</summary>

<a id="eq:continuum-residual-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-hole-norms"></a>
*Proof.* Since $h=h_i-\sum_{k\ne i}V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}$, $$A\psi_i=\epsilon_i\psi_i-
       \sum_{k\ne i}V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i.
 \label{eq:continuum-residual-identity}$$ The $k$-th hole is supported in $r_k\le D/8$. On it, $$r_i\ge 7D/8-2S\quad(k\ne i).$$ Both Coulomb poles of $V_k$ lie in this hole for large $D$. The charges $q,q'$ are bounded, and direct radial integration, enlarging the integration ball by at most four for the secondary pole, gives $$\|V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}\|_2\le C D^{1/2},
 \qquad
 \|V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}\|_1\le C D^2.
 \label{eq:continuum-hole-norms}$$ Consequently the sum in <a href="#eq:continuum-residual-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-residual-identity">[eq:continuum-residual-identity]</a> has norm at most $$CmD^{1/2}\exp(-.85635D+1.98S).$$ Together with the tuning error, this is at most $e^{-.75D}$ for the stated regime and sufficiently large $D$.

For $i\ne j$, put $\ell_{ij}=|X_i-X_j|$. The product of the two tail bounds is at most $$C\exp\bigl(-.99(r_i+r_j)+.0198D\bigr).$$ Use $r_i+r_j\ge \ell_{ij}$ on a factor with exponent $.90$; the remaining factor is integrable uniformly in the centers, since $\int e^{-.09(r_i+r_j)}\,\,\mathrm dy\le
\int e^{-.09r_i}\,\,\mathrm dy\le C$. Thus $$|G_{ij}|\le C e^{-.90\ell_{ij}+.0198D}
 \le C e^{-.8802D+1.8S}.$$ The diagonal entries are one. The row-sum estimate and $m+S\le D^{1/4}$ prove the second inequality in <a href="#eq:continuum-residual-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-residual-gram">[eq:continuum-residual-gram]</a>.

Let $M=\mathcal U^*A\mathcal U$, which is real symmetric. We next estimate its entries before orthonormalization. If $k\ne i,j$, both $r_i$ and $r_j$ are at least $7D/8-2S$ on the $k$-th hole. Equations <a href="#eq:continuum-tail-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-tail-input">[eq:continuum-tail-input]</a> and <a href="#eq:continuum-hole-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-hole-norms">[eq:continuum-hole-norms]</a> therefore imply $$\sum_{k\ne i,j}
 |\langle\psi_j,V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i\rangle|
 \le CmD^2e^{-1.7127D+3.96S}.$$ The same estimate bounds $\sum_{k\ne i}\langle\psi_i,V_k^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i\rangle$. For a noncontact pair, the remaining hole at $j$ is bounded using $r_i+r_j\ge\sqrt2D-2S$, giving $$|\langle\psi_j,V_j^{\mathrm{hole}}\psi_i\rangle|
 \le CD^2
 e^{-(.99\sqrt2-.0198)D+1.98S}.$$ Here $.99\sqrt2-.0198>1.38$. All these bounds are smaller than $e^{-1.1D}$, after absorbing their polynomial factors and fixed constants. For $i\ne j$, the term $\epsilon_iG_{ji}$ is at most $e^{-1.86D}$. It follows, adjusting a fixed constant, that <a id="eq:continuum-raw-entries"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |M_{ii}-\epsilon_i|&\le Ce^{-1.1D},\notag\\
 |M_{ji}+B_{ij}|&\le Ce^{-1.1D}
       &&(i\to j\text{ a chosen contact}),\label{eq:continuum-raw-entries}\\
 |M_{ij}|&\le Ce^{-1.1D}
       &&(i\ne j,\ \{i,j\}\notin\mathcal C).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ The exactly symmetric matrix $M$ requires only one of the two orientations of each contact. Its diagonal tuning contribution has norm at most $e^{-1.01D}$, not $m e^{-1.01D}$. Entrywise comparison with $\mathsf T$ and a row-sum bound now give $$\|Z_*M-\mathsf T\|
 \le m\epsilon_{\mathrm{hop}}
     +Z_*e^{-1.01D}+CmZ_*e^{-1.1D}.$$

For completeness, the residual estimate controls the entire matrix, without using a dimension-dependent bound on a many-body space: $$\|A\mathcal U\|\le\sqrt m\,e^{-.75D},\qquad
 \|\mathcal U\|=\|G\|^{1/2}\le2,\qquad
 \|M\|\le C\sqrt m\,e^{-.75D}.$$ Functional calculus on the positive matrix $G$ gives $\|G^{-1/2}-I\|\le Ce^{-.85D}$ and $\|G^{-1/2}\|\le2$. Consequently <a id="eq:continuum-gram-correction"></a>$$\|G^{-1/2}MG^{-1/2}-M\|
 \le C\sqrt m\,e^{-1.6D}
 \le Cm e^{-1.6D}.
 \label{eq:continuum-gram-correction}$$ This orthonormalizes the matrix of the shifted operator $h+1/2$. In the original nonorthogonal modes, $M=\mathcal U^*h\mathcal U+\tfrac12G$. The shift is essential in this estimate: one must add $\tfrac12G$, rather than $\tfrac12I$, before orthonormalization. Equation <a href="#eq:continuum-one-body-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-one-body-error">[eq:continuum-one-body-error]</a> follows.

The same residual estimate applied to $A\mathcal J_0=A\mathcal U G^{-1/2}$ proves the first part of <a href="#eq:continuum-projected-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-projected-residual">[eq:continuum-projected-residual]</a>. Finally, as a map into $H^1$, $\mathcal U$ has norm at most $C\sqrt m$, by <a href="#eq:continuum-orbital-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-orbital-input">[eq:continuum-orbital-input]</a>. Thus $$\|\omega_i-\psi_i\|_{H^1}
 \le C\sqrt m\,e^{-.85D}.$$ Combining this with $\|\psi_i-\phi_i\|_{H^1}\le Cm/D$ gives the remaining assertion. ◻

</details>

We now include spin. Set $$\mathfrak h=L^2(\mathbb R^3;\mathbb C^2),\qquad
 P=P_0\otimes I_2,\qquad
 \mathcal J=\mathcal J_0\otimes I_2.$$ Thus $P_0$ has rank $m$, whereas $P$ has rank $2m$. The operator $A$ acts trivially on spin; we use the same symbol for this extension. Its residual and matrix bounds are unchanged.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A gap on the entire one-particle complement

Spatial localization separates the nuclei from the exterior, where the attraction is uniformly weak. We use the IMS product-rule identity (compare Simon (Simon 1983, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Simon1983](#ref-Simon1983)]); its kinetic-error coefficient is $1/2$ for our normalization $-\Delta/2$. The identity is derived below. The additional point to verify is that a globally orthogonal function has only exponentially small components along the local ground states after localization.

<a id="stmt-prop-complement-gap"></a>
