<div id="prop:complement-gap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Continuum complement gap). *There is a constant $g>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $D$, $$(I-P)A(I-P)\ge g(I-P).$$ The inequality holds on the full one-particle form domain, including functions supported between the nuclei or at infinity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-complement-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-complement-gap</summary>

<a id="eq:continuum-ims-gradients"></a>
*Proof.* Choose smooth real functions $\theta_0,\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_m$ with $$\sum_{a=0}^m\theta_a^2=1,\qquad
 \theta_i=1\ \text{on }r_i\le D/5,\qquad
 \operatorname{supp}\theta_i\subseteq\{r_i\le D/4\}.$$ The balls of radius $D/4$ are disjoint by <a href="#eq:continuum-separations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-separations">[eq:continuum-separations]</a>. In the annulus $D/5<r_i<D/4$, take $\theta_i=\cos f(r_i/D)$ and $\theta_0=\sin f(r_i/D)$, where $f$ is a fixed smooth transition from zero to $\pi/2$, constant near both endpoints. Set $\theta_0=0$ in the inner balls and one outside the outer balls. At most one transition annulus is present at any point, so $$\sum_{a=0}^m|\nabla\theta_a|^2\le CD^{-2}.
 \label{eq:continuum-ims-gradients}$$ This construction also verifies smoothness at the boundaries.

<a id="eq:continuum-local-ground-leakage"></a>
Let $u\in H^1(\mathbb R^3;\mathbb C^2)$, $Pu=0$, and $\|u\|=1$. In spin component $\sigma$, global orthogonality implies $$b_{i\sigma}:=\langle\psi_i,\theta_i u_\sigma\rangle
 =\langle(\theta_i-1)\psi_i,u_\sigma\rangle.$$ The tail estimate, integrated over $r_i\ge D/5$, gives $$\|(\theta_i-1)\psi_i\|
 \le C\operatorname{poly}(D)e^{-.99(.2-.01)D}
 \le e^{-.18D}.$$ Here the polynomial comes only from radial integration and has fixed degree. Consequently $$\sum_{i,\sigma}|b_{i\sigma}|^2\le me^{-.36D}.
 \label{eq:continuum-local-ground-leakage}$$ There is no assumption that a localized vector is exactly orthogonal to the local ground state.

On $\operatorname{supp}\theta_i$, every $k\ne i$ satisfies $$r_k\ge D-2S-D/4=3D/4-2S>D/8.$$ Hence $\chi_k=1$ throughout that support and $h=h_i$ there. Choose a fixed $0<g_{\mathrm{loc}}\le\min(g_{\mathrm w}/2,1/4)$. Equations <a href="#eq:continuum-well-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-well-gap">[eq:continuum-well-gap]</a> and <a href="#eq:continuum-tail-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-tail-input">[eq:continuum-tail-input]</a>, for large $D$, imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle\theta_i u,A\theta_i u\rangle
 &\ge g_{\mathrm{loc}}\|\theta_i u\|^2
   -(g_{\mathrm{loc}}+e^{-1.01D})\sum_\sigma|b_{i\sigma}|^2 .
\end{aligned}$$

On $\operatorname{supp}\theta_0$, each primary distance is at least $D/5$. Each secondary distance is at least $D/5-\rho_i\ge D/5-4\ge D/6$. Since $q+q'\le2$ for large $D$, the magnitude of the full attraction on this support is at most $Cm/D$. Thus $$\langle\theta_0u,A\theta_0u\rangle
 \ge(1/2-Cm/D)\|\theta_0u\|^2
 \ge g_{\mathrm{loc}}\|\theta_0u\|^2.$$

The kinetic product rule yields the IMS identity $$\langle u,Au\rangle
 =\sum_{a=0}^m\langle\theta_au,A\theta_au\rangle
 -\frac12\int\sum_{a=0}^m|\nabla\theta_a|^2|u|^2.$$ It holds first for smooth functions and then on $H^1$ by continuity of the Coulomb forms. Summing the preceding bounds and using <a href="#eq:continuum-ims-gradients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-ims-gradients">[eq:continuum-ims-gradients]</a> and <a href="#eq:continuum-local-ground-leakage" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-local-ground-leakage">[eq:continuum-local-ground-leakage]</a> gives $$\langle u,Au\rangle
 \ge g_{\mathrm{loc}}-Cme^{-.36D}-CD^{-2}\ge g_{\mathrm{loc}}/2.$$ Taking $g=g_{\mathrm{loc}}/2$ proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Uniform many-fermion bounds and elimination of high modes

Let $$\mathfrak H_m=\bigwedge\nolimits^m\mathfrak h,\qquad
 \Pi=\bigwedge\nolimits^mP,\qquad Q_m=I-\Pi .$$ Here $\Pi$ projects onto *all* $m$-electron configurations in the $2m$ spin orbitals; it does not impose single occupancy or a total-spin condition. Denote second quantization on this fixed particle-number space by $d\Gamma(B)=\sum_{\ell=1}^mB^{(\ell)}$. In the scaled coordinates, write $W_{\mathrm{ee}}=\sum_{1\le\ell<k\le m}|y_\ell-y_k|^{-1}$ for the electron repulsion, acting trivially on spin.

<a id="eq:continuum-scaled-hamiltonian"></a>
Under the unitary dilation $y_\ell=Z_*x_\ell$, the physical Hamiltonian obeys the exact relation <a id="eq:continuum-exact-dilation"></a>$$\frac{H}{Z_*^2}
 =d\Gamma(h)+\frac{W_{\mathrm{ee}}}{Z_*}.
 \label{eq:continuum-exact-dilation}$$ We henceforth use this unitary identification and set $$\mathcal H=\frac{H}{Z_*^2}+\frac m2
 =d\Gamma(A)+\frac{W_{\mathrm{ee}}}{Z_*}.
 \label{eq:continuum-scaled-hamiltonian}$$ To compare its spectral infimum with its compression to $\Pi$, we will bound the gap on $Q_m$ and the coupling $Q_m\mathcal H\Pi$. The one-particle estimates control the contribution from $d\Gamma(A)$; the following Hardy estimate controls the repulsion on every vector in $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$.

We briefly specify the domains used below. On the antisymmetric subspace, the kinetic form domain is $H^1$ in all $3m$ coordinates, with its finite spin components. Its operator domain is $H^2$ in those coordinates. Translated Hardy inequalities in one particle coordinate show that each nuclear or pair Coulomb multiplier is bounded from $H^1$ to $L^2$. For a fixed instance their finite sum therefore satisfies $\|Vu\|_2\le C_{\rm inst}\|u\|_{H^1}$. The Fourier inequality $\|\nabla u\|_2\le\varepsilon\|\Delta u\|_2+
C_\varepsilon\|u\|_2$ makes the potential infinitesimally operator-bounded relative to the total Laplacian. The Coulomb operator consequently has the indicated $H^2$ operator domain and $H^1$ form domain. The same reasoning applies to the one-particle operators. Every $\omega_i$ is in $H^2(\mathbb R^3)$. Products of these functions have square-integrable pure second derivatives and mixed first derivatives, so every vector in $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$ belongs to the many-particle operator domain. Both $\Pi$ and $Q_m$ preserve the form domain.

<a id="stmt-lem-fermion-hardy"></a>
<div id="lem:fermion-hardy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (A dimension-independent fermionic Hardy bound). *Uniformly over all normalized $u\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$, <a id="eq:continuum-fermion-hardy"></a>$$\sum_{\ell=1}^m\|\nabla_{y_\ell}u\|_2^2\le Cm,
 \qquad
 \|W_{\mathrm{ee}}\Pi\|\le Cm^2.
 \label{eq:continuum-fermion-hardy}$$ The constants do not depend on the dimension $\binom{2m}{m}$ of $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fermion-hardy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fermion-hardy</summary>

*Proof.* Let $(K_{\mathrm{raw}})_{ij}
=\langle\nabla\psi_i,\nabla\psi_j\rangle$. This is positive semidefinite, with trace at most $Cm$. In the orthonormal spatial modes the kinetic matrix is $$K_0=G^{-1/2}K_{\mathrm{raw}}G^{-1/2}.$$ Hence $$\operatorname{Tr}K_0
 =\operatorname{Tr}(K_{\mathrm{raw}}G^{-1})
 \le\|G^{-1}\|\operatorname{Tr}K_{\mathrm{raw}}\le Cm.$$ The spin matrix $K=K_0\otimes I_2$ has the same bound after adjusting the constant.

For any normalized fermionic $u$, its one-particle density matrix $\gamma$ in these modes satisfies $0\le\gamma\le I$. Indeed, for a coefficient vector $v$, the quadratic form of $\gamma$ is $\|c(v)u\|^2$; the anticommutation relation $c(v)c(v)^\dagger+c(v)^\dagger c(v)=\|v\|^2I$ places it between zero and $\|v\|^2$. Thus, for an arbitrary superposition in the finite-mode space, $$\sum_\ell\|\nabla_{y_\ell}u\|_2^2
 =\operatorname{Tr}(\gamma K)\le\operatorname{Tr}K\le Cm.$$

For a pair $\ell<k$, applying the three-dimensional Hardy inequality in $y_\ell$, with $y_k$ and all other variables held fixed, gives $$\|\,|y_\ell-y_k|^{-1}u\|_2
 \le2\|\nabla_{y_\ell}u\|_2.$$ Integration over the remaining variables and spin components justifies this inequality for the full wavefunction. Put $b_m=m(m-1)/2$. Cauchy–Schwarz in the sum of pair multipliers gives, when $m\ge2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|W_{\mathrm{ee}}u\|_2^2
 &\le b_m\sum_{\ell<k}
          \|\,|y_\ell-y_k|^{-1}u\|_2^2\\
 &\le4b_m\sum_{\ell<k}\|\nabla_{y_\ell}u\|_2^2\\
 &\le4b_m(m-1)\sum_\ell\|\nabla_{y_\ell}u\|_2^2
 \le Cm^4.
\end{aligned}$$ For $m=1$ the repulsion is zero. This proves the operator bound on the whole finite-mode space, rather than only on its individual Slater determinants. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-continuum-elimination"></a>
<div id="lem:continuum-elimination" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Eliminating the many-particle complement). *For sufficiently large $D$, let $E_\Pi=\min\operatorname{Spec}
(\Pi\mathcal H\Pi|_{\operatorname{Ran}\Pi})$. Then <a id="eq:continuum-leakage-error"></a>$$0\le E_\Pi-\inf\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal H
 \le e^{-1.2D}.
 \label{eq:continuum-leakage-error}$$ More explicitly, the upper bound can be taken to be $$C\left(m^{3/2}e^{-.75D}+m^2/Z_*\right)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-continuum-elimination"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-continuum-elimination</summary>

<a id="eq:continuum-high-number-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\varepsilon=\|AP\|\le C\sqrt m\,e^{-.75D}$. The one-particle off-block operator $$R_{\mathrm{off}}=PA(I-P)+(I-P)AP$$ extends to a bounded self-adjoint operator, of norm $\|(I-P)AP\|\le\varepsilon$. The adjoint identifies the two blocks even though $A$ itself is unbounded. Its block-diagonal remainder satisfies $$A-R_{\mathrm{off}}
 =PAP+(I-P)A(I-P)\ge-\varepsilon P+g(I-P)$$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:complement-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:complement-gap">24</a>. Consequently, on the full many-particle form domain, $$d\Gamma(A)\ge gN_\perp-2m\varepsilon I,
 \qquad N_\perp=d\Gamma(I-P).
 \label{eq:continuum-high-number-bound}$$ Here the low diagonal can cost at most $m\varepsilon$, and $\|d\Gamma(R_{\mathrm{off}})\|\le m\varepsilon$. The commuting one-particle projections $(I-P)^{(\ell)}$ show that $N_\perp$ has nonnegative integer eigenvalues, with kernel exactly $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$. Therefore $N_\perp\ge Q_m$, including on superpositions of states with different numbers of high-mode particles.

The repulsion form is nonnegative on every wavefunction, so its compression to $Q_m$ is nonnegative even though $W_{\mathrm{ee}}$ need not commute with $\Pi$. Equation <a href="#eq:continuum-high-number-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-high-number-bound">[eq:continuum-high-number-bound]</a> gives <a id="eq:continuum-many-body-gap"></a>$$Q_m\mathcal H Q_m\ge(g-2m\varepsilon)Q_m
 \ge cQ_m,\qquad c=g/2,
 \label{eq:continuum-many-body-gap}$$ for large $D$.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:fermion-hardy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fermion-hardy">25</a>, the coupling from the finite subspace has operator norm at most <a id="eq:continuum-coupling"></a>$$\beta:=\|Q_m\mathcal H\Pi\|
 \le m\varepsilon+\frac{Cm^2}{Z_*}
 \le Cm^{3/2}e^{-.75D}+\frac{Cm^2}{Z_*}.
 \label{eq:continuum-coupling}$$ The first term follows because the block-diagonal one-particle sum preserves $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$. The compressed operator also obeys $$\|\Pi\mathcal H\Pi\|\le m\varepsilon+Cm^2/Z_*=o(1).$$

For any form-domain vector $u=p+v$, where $p=\Pi u$ and $v=Q_m u$, the finite-mode operator-domain inclusion and the bounded coupling justify $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle u,\mathcal H u\rangle
 &\ge\langle p,\mathcal H p\rangle
       +c\|v\|^2-2\beta\|p\|\|v\|\\
 &\ge\langle p,\mathcal H p\rangle
       -\frac{2\beta^2}{c}\|p\|^2+\frac c2\|v\|^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, this is the form inequality $$\mathcal H\ge
 \Pi\mathcal H\Pi-\frac{2\beta^2}{c}\Pi+\frac c2Q_m.$$ Since the finite block has norm $o(1)$, the bottom of the right side is $E_\Pi-2\beta^2/c$ for large $D$. The opposite variational inequality follows by restricting test vectors to $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$. This proves the explicit error bound. In the present regime, with $Z_*\ge e^D/2$, it is at most $e^{-1.2D}$. All lower bounds apply to quadratic forms and hence to spectral infima; existence of an eigenvector at the bottom of the full continuum spectrum is not used. ◻

</details>

In particular, the explicit error in this lemma, after multiplication by $Z_*$, is bounded by <a id="eq:continuum-leakage-final-units"></a>$$C\left(
    m^3e^{-.5D}+m^{7/2}e^{-.75D}+m^4e^{-D}
 \right).
 \label{eq:continuum-leakage-final-units}$$ The weaker bound $Z_*e^{-1.2D}\le2e^{-.2D}$ will suffice. This squaring of the residual against the fixed complement gap is what permits the final energy amplification.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The compressed Coulomb interaction

The full-space energy is now controlled by its finite-mode compression, with an error that remains small after multiplication by $Z_*$. To identify that compression we retain the direct intersite repulsion and estimate it explicitly. At the present exponentially amplified scales it has norm $O(m^2/D)$ in Hubbard units, unlike constructions that must keep it at leading order at polynomial confinement scales.

Identify $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$ with $\bigwedge^m(\mathbb C^m\otimes\mathbb C^2)$ using the isometry $\mathcal J_m=\bigwedge^m\mathcal J$. Let $$W_{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal J_m^*W_{\mathrm{ee}}\mathcal J_m.$$ Creation and annihilation operators below refer to these orthonormal abstract site modes.

<a id="stmt-lem-continuum-coulomb-compression"></a>
<div id="lem:continuum-coulomb-compression" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Coulomb coefficients in the site modes). *For sufficiently large $D$, <a id="eq:continuum-coulomb-error"></a>$$\left\|W_{\mathrm{eff}}
       -U\sum_{i=1}^m n_{i\uparrow}n_{i\downarrow}\right\|
 \le \frac{Cm^5}{D},\qquad U=\frac58 .
 \label{eq:continuum-coulomb-error}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-continuum-coulomb-compression"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-continuum-coulomb-compression</summary>

<a id="eq:continuum-coefficient-continuity"></a>
*Proof.* For four spatial functions define $$\mathcal V(a,b;c,d)=
 \iint\frac{\overline{a(x)b(y)}\,c(x)d(y)}
                {|x-y|}\,\,\mathrm dx\,\,\mathrm dy.$$ Hardy in the $x$ coordinate and Cauchy–Schwarz show that $$|\mathcal V(a,b;c,d)|
 \le2\|a\|_2\|b\|_2\|\nabla c\|_2\|d\|_2.
 \label{eq:continuum-coefficient-continuity}$$ Telescoping a change of the four arguments therefore makes this coefficient Lipschitz in their $H^1$ norms on uniformly bounded $H^1$ sets. The same statement holds for fixed spin orbitals, with the usual spin Kronecker factors.

The coefficients of $W_{\mathrm{eff}}$ are these integrals in the modes $\omega_i$ and their two spin copies. Equation <a href="#eq:continuum-projected-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-projected-residual">[eq:continuum-projected-residual]</a> permits their replacement by $\phi_i$ with error at most $Cm/D$ per coefficient. Indeed all four $H^1$ norms stay uniformly bounded. In the standard second-quantized expression each coefficient multiplies $\tfrac12c_a^\dagger c_b^\dagger c_d c_c$, a monomial of norm at most one. There are at most $(2m)^4$ coefficients. Thus this replacement changes the operator by at most $Cm^5/D$. We are comparing coefficient arrays in the fixed abstract orthonormal site space; no orthogonality of the replacement functions $\phi_i$ is asserted or needed.

Choose a fixed smooth radial cutoff equal to one on $r_i\le D/5$, zero on $r_i\ge D/4$, and with gradient bounded by $C/D$, and let $\zeta_i$ be its product with $\phi_i$. Direct integration of the hydrogenic tail gives $$\|\zeta_i-\phi_i\|_{H^1}
 \le C\operatorname{poly}(D)e^{-D/5}\le e^{-.18D}.$$ The supports of the $\zeta_i$ are disjoint. By <a href="#eq:continuum-coefficient-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-coefficient-continuity">[eq:continuum-coefficient-continuity]</a>, replacing all $\phi_i$ by $\zeta_i$ changes the coefficient operator by at most $Cm^4e^{-.18D}$.

For the truncated functions a nonzero coefficient must have the same site label for both factors at $x$, and likewise at $y$. Therefore the only surviving terms are onsite and intersite density terms. If $i\ne j$, their spatial coefficient satisfies $$0\le V_{ij}^{\mathrm{dir}}
 =\iint\frac{|\zeta_i(x)|^2|\zeta_j(y)|^2}{|x-y|}
                  \,\,\mathrm dx\,\,\mathrm dy
 \le\frac{1}{D/2-2S}\le\frac CD,$$ using $\|\zeta_i\|_2\le1$. The intersite operator is $\sum_{i<j}V_{ij}^{\mathrm{dir}}n_i n_j$, where $n_i=n_{i\uparrow}+n_{i\downarrow}$ has norm two. It consequently has norm at most $Cm^2/D$.

The onsite spatial integral differs from $\mathcal V(\phi_i,\phi_i;\phi_i,\phi_i)=U$ by at most $Ce^{-.18D}$. The two equal-spin monomials vanish by anticommutation. The two opposite-spin monomials, together with the factor $1/2$ in the interaction, give exactly $U n_{i\uparrow}n_{i\downarrow}$. Combining these estimates yields $$\left\|W_{\mathrm{eff}}
       -U\sum_i n_{i\uparrow}n_{i\downarrow}\right\|
 \le C\left(\frac{m^5}{D}
       +m^4e^{-.18D}+\frac{m^2}{D}\right)
 \le\frac{Cm^5}{D}.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The simulation estimate in physical energy units

<a id="stmt-prop-continuum-simulation"></a>
<div id="prop:continuum-simulation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:continuum-simulation-units"></a>
<a id="eq:continuum-simulation-final"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Simulation of the Hubbard spectral minimum). *Let $E_0=\inf\operatorname{Spec}H$ for the physical molecular Hamiltonian and let $E_{\mathrm{Hub}}(t)$ be the minimum of the half-filled Hubbard Hamiltonian in Proposition <a href="#prop:hubbard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hubbard">7</a>. The construction satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\frac{E_0}{Z_*}+\frac{mZ_*}{2}
                   -E_{\mathrm{Hub}}(t)\right|
 &\le m\delta_1+\frac{Cm^5}{D}+Z_*e^{-1.2D}
       \label{eq:continuum-simulation-units}\\
 &\le C\biggl[
       m^2\epsilon_{\mathrm{hop}}+\frac{m^5}{D}
       +m e^{-.01D}+m^2e^{-.1D}\notag\\
 &\hspace{35mm}{}+m^2e^{-.6D}+e^{-.2D}
       \biggr].\label{eq:continuum-simulation-final}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, for every prescribed fixed inverse-polynomial error budget $\epsilon_*$, the polynomial scale and calibration precision can be chosen so that the left side is at most $\epsilon_*$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-continuum-simulation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-continuum-simulation</summary>

*Proof.* The exact compression, in the abstract site space, is $$\mathcal J_m^*(Z_*\mathcal H)\mathcal J_m
 =d\Gamma\bigl(Z_*\mathcal J^*A\mathcal J\bigr)
    +W_{\mathrm{eff}}.$$ The Hubbard hopping term is $d\Gamma(\mathsf T\otimes I_2)$. On $m$ particles, second quantization of a bounded one-particle difference increases its norm by at most a factor $m$. Lemma <a href="#lem:one-body-matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:one-body-matrix">23</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:continuum-coulomb-compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:continuum-coulomb-compression">27</a> therefore give $$\left\|\mathcal J_m^*(Z_*\mathcal H)\mathcal J_m
             -H_{\mathrm{Hub}}(t)\right\|
 \le m\delta_1+Cm^5/D.$$ For finite self-adjoint matrices, the difference of their spectral minima is bounded by the operator norm difference, by the variational principle. Lemma <a href="#lem:continuum-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:continuum-elimination">26</a> adds at most $Z_*e^{-1.2D}$ when the restriction to $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi$ is removed. Finally, <a href="#eq:continuum-scaled-hamiltonian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-scaled-hamiltonian">[eq:continuum-scaled-hamiltonian]</a> gives the exact energy identity $$Z_*\inf\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal H
 =\frac{E_0}{Z_*}+\frac{mZ_*}{2}.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:continuum-simulation-units" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-simulation-units">[eq:continuum-simulation-units]</a>. Substitute <a href="#eq:continuum-one-body-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-one-body-error">[eq:continuum-one-body-error]</a> and $Z_*\le2e^D$ to obtain <a href="#eq:continuum-simulation-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-simulation-final">[eq:continuum-simulation-final]</a>.

The right side involves only fixed polynomial factors in $m$, the calibrated hopping error, inverse powers of $D$, and the displayed decaying exponentials. Equation <a href="#eq:continuum-calibration-interface" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-calibration-interface">[eq:continuum-calibration-interface]</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:calibration">22</a> allow the hopping error to be made an arbitrarily small fixed inverse polynomial. The simultaneous choices, their order, and polynomial bit cost are specified in Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">6</a>. No selected-orbital assumption has been imposed on the spectral infimum in this comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Parameters, computation, and the final promise

We now put the constructions in their algorithmic order. This matters because the tail formulas are proved for tuned wells, whereas the positions must be computed before the tuning is performed. The surrogate fields in <a href="#sec:hopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hopping">4</a> remove this apparent dependence.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### A uniform choice of scales

Let $n\ge2$ bound the source input size, with the fixed polynomial magnitude and inverse-gap bounds from <a href="#thm:source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:source">2</a>. Write $E_{\rm in}=E(H_{\rm in})$. Put $$\delta_s=b_s-a_s,\qquad \gamma=\min\{1,\delta_s\},
\qquad \alpha=\eta^2/U.$$ Use the scales and rational hopping approximation of <a href="#prop:hubbard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hubbard">7</a>. They give <a id="eq:finite-final-error"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-final-error}
\left|E_{\rm Hub}(t)-\alpha(E_{\rm in}-C_s)\right|
\le \frac{3\alpha\gamma}{64},
\qquad
C_s=3k\Delta+\frac32\sum_e v_e^2+
                      \sum_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal C}K_{ij}.$$ All parameters chosen so far have polynomial magnitude or inverse-polynomial lower bounds as appropriate. Their binary descriptions have polynomial length. The scale $\alpha$ is rational. The scalar $C_s$ is a sum of polynomially many efficiently computable square roots and rational terms; it does not depend on $E_{\rm in}$.

<a id="eq:continuum-budget"></a>
<a id="eq:uniform-displacement-size"></a>
<a id="eq:calibration-budget"></a>
Set the requested continuum error to $$\label{eq:continuum-budget}
\epsilon_* = \frac{\alpha\gamma}{64}.$$ We verify that a single deterministic polynomial choice of $D$ meets this budget. The calibrated displacement bound has the form $$\label{eq:uniform-displacement-size}
S\le C n^b(1+\log D)$$ for a fixed $b$, since $m$, the number of contacts, and $|\log t_{ij}|$ are polynomially bounded in $n$. Increase $b$ so that $m\le Cn^b$ as well. The hopping error before polynomial norm conversions is bounded by a constant times $$\label{eq:calibration-budget}
\begin{split}
\mathfrak e(D,\tau)={}&
\frac{m\log D+m^2}{D}+\frac{mS}{D}
+\frac{S^2}{D}+S D^{-1/4}\\
&+\tau+e^{-c\sqrt D}.
\end{split}$$ The other errors are $\operatorname{poly}(m)/D$ and the exponentially small terms listed below. Choose fixed exponents $h,r$ so that all polynomial norm-conversion and coefficient-counting losses are bounded by $Cn^h$, and $\epsilon_*\ge c n^{-r}$. In particular, take $m^2+m^5\le Cn^h$; the two algebraic error terms in <a href="#eq:continuum-simulation-final" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:continuum-simulation-final">[eq:continuum-simulation-final]</a> are then bounded by $Cn^h(\mathfrak e(D,\tau)+D^{-1})$. Such exponents exist by the explicit finite construction. Increasing them only weakens the conditions to be met.

<a id="eq:choice-K"></a>
<a id="eq:choice-D"></a>
Choose an integer $K$ satisfying $$\label{eq:choice-K}
K>4(b+h+r+2),$$ and put $$\label{eq:choice-D}
D=A(n+2)^K,
\qquad
\tau=c_*(n+2)^{-h-r-2},$$ where $A$ is a sufficiently large fixed integer and $c_*>0$ a sufficiently small fixed rational. The factors $1+\log D$ in <a href="#eq:uniform-displacement-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:uniform-displacement-size">[eq:uniform-displacement-size]</a> do not make this choice circular: $K$ is chosen first, and then $A$ is fixed. Each $D$-dependent expression tends to zero relative to its error budget as $A$ increases, even with its powers of $\log A$. The remaining growth in $n$ is controlled by <a href="#eq:choice-K" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:choice-K">[eq:choice-K]</a>. In particular, $$n^h S D^{-1/4}=o(n^{-r}),\qquad
n^h S^2/D=o(n^{-r}),\qquad
m+S=o(D^{1/4}).$$ Here the little-oh statements describe the uniform large-scale estimates used to choose the fixed constants; increasing $A$ covers every finite small-$n$ case. The same choices bound all the terms in <a href="#eq:calibration-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:calibration-budget">[eq:calibration-budget]</a> and the Coulomb-compression error by their assigned fractions of <a href="#eq:continuum-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:continuum-budget">[eq:continuum-budget]</a>. No search for a successful $D$ is part of the algorithm.

It is important to keep the energy units explicit. After dilation and subtraction of the isolated-well ground energy, <a id="eq:units-final"></a>$$\label{eq:units-final}
\mathcal H=H/Z_*^2+m/2,
\qquad Z_*\mathcal H=H/Z_*+mZ_*/2.$$ The exponential margins that are needed after multiplication by $Z_*\asymp e^D$ are:

<div class="center">

| Source of error            | In $\mathcal H$ units             | In $Z_*\mathcal H$ units         |
|:---------------------------|:----------------------------------|:---------------------------------|
| Tuned well energies        | $m e^{-1.01D}$                    | $O(m e^{-.01D})$                 |
| Discarded one-body entries | $O(m^2 e^{-1.1D})$                | $O(m^2 e^{-.1D})$                |
| Orthonormalization         | $\operatorname{poly}(m)e^{-1.6D}$ | $\operatorname{poly}(m)e^{-.6D}$ |
| Continuum complement       | $e^{-1.2D}$                       | $O(e^{-.2D})$                    |

</div>

The polynomial multiplicities are retained in <a href="#eq:continuum-simulation-final" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:continuum-simulation-final">[eq:continuum-simulation-final]</a>. The positive exponential margins allow all these terms to fit their assigned error budgets by increasing the fixed $A$. The one-body residual $e^{-.75D}$ is used quadratically against a fixed complementary gap; multiplying that residual linearly by $Z_*$ would not be a valid estimate. [prop:continuum-simulation](#prop:continuum-simulation) supplies the required quadratic bound.

With these choices, that proposition gives <a id="eq:full-final-error"></a>$$\label{eq:full-final-error}
\left|E_0/Z_*+mZ_*/2-\alpha(E_{\rm in}-C_s)\right|
\le \frac{\alpha\gamma}{16}
\le \frac{\alpha\delta_s}{16}.$$

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The deterministic construction and its bit cost

First check the source encoding and the fixed polynomial bounds in <a href="#thm:source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:source">2</a>, including the inverse-gap bound. If a check fails, output the fixed instance with one electron, one unit-charge nucleus at the origin, and thresholds $-1/2$ and $1/2$. Otherwise carry out the following steps. The guard is polynomial-time and makes the reduction a total polynomial-time map; it never rejects a promised source instance.

1.  *Finite models.* Read the signed rational Heisenberg instance. Form the ancillary graph, the algebraic reference positions $p_i$, the complete contact set $\mathcal C$, the explicit scales $\Delta,\eta$, and the positive rational hoppings $t_{ij}$ of <a href="#prop:hubbard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hubbard">7</a>. The finite contact classification avoids deciding arbitrary geometric equalities numerically. All square roots here are approximated with certified rational intervals to the stated inverse-polynomial errors.

2.  *Charge scale.* Choose $D$ as in <a href="#eq:choice-D" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:choice-D">[eq:choice-D]</a> and compute a positive integer $Z_*$ with $$e^D/2\le Z_*\le2e^D.$$ This does not require an exact comparison with a transcendental number. For example, compute the rational sum $s_D=\sum_{j=0}^{4D}D^j/j!$ and take $Z_*=\lfloor s_D\rfloor$. The ratio of successive omitted terms is at most $1/4$, and $j!\ge(j/e)^j$, so $0<e^D-s_D<1$ for sufficiently large $D$. Thus this choice has the required bounds. The sum has polynomially many terms and polynomial bit cost. Round $Z_*(1-500m/D)$ and $Z_*1000m/D$ to the positive integer primary and secondary charges. They are fixed before any energy tuning.

3.  *Positions.* At the reference arrangement $X_i^0=Dp_i$, compute each positive surrogate hopping integral to the required relative accuracy, then compute $$d_{ij}=\log\bigl(Z_*T_{ij}(X^0)/t_{ij}\bigr)$$ to the prescribed inverse-polynomial error. Apply the explicit right inverse from <a href="#lem:displacement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:displacement">6</a> and round the resulting scaled centers to rational $X_i$. The final displacements are defined by $s_i=X_i-Dp_i$; thus the exact contact-vector decomposition is retained after rounding. [prop:calibration](#prop:calibration) proves that these operations take polynomial time and satisfy the required projected-displacement tolerances. The calculation uses $W_i^*$ and is independent of the unknown tuned parameters.

4.  *Well tuning.* With these final rational centers and integer charges fixed, apply the certified one-well oracle and the contraction iteration of <a href="#prop:tuning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuning">14</a>. This produces rational $\rho_i\in[1,4]$ such that $|\lambda_i+1/2|\le e^{-1.01D}$. No subsequent change to a charge or center is made.

5.  *Physical instance.* Output the $2m$ nuclei at $$R_{i,0}=X_i/Z_*,\qquad
    R_{i,1}=(X_i+\rho_i e_z)/Z_*$$ with their positive integer charges, and output $N=m$ in unary. Compute the rational thresholds specified in the next subsection.

Each step is classical and deterministic. The one-well Ritz spaces have dimension polynomial in $D$ and in the rational coordinate bit lengths. Their matrix entries and conditioning bounds require only polynomially many bits, and the number of eigenvalue evaluations in the tuning procedure is polynomial. The integral evaluations and elementary functions in calibration likewise require only polynomial time and precision. In particular, no many-electron ground energy is queried.

The output has polynomial length. Each nuclear charge has $O(D+\log n)$ bits. Rational scaled centers and secondary offsets have polynomial bit length, and division by the $O(D)$-bit integer $Z_*$ preserves that property. Distinct sites are separated by $D-O(S)$ in scaled coordinates; each secondary nucleus is between one and four units from its own primary. Thus all output nuclei are distinct for the chosen $D$. Reducing rational coordinates and thresholds to lowest terms is a polynomial-time integer computation. Finally, $m$ is polynomial in $n$, so unary encoding of $N$ has polynomial length as well. Auxiliary caps, orbitals, Ritz bases, and projections are used in the proof and computation only; none is part of the output instance.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Rational thresholds and preservation of both promises

Compute a rational $\widetilde C_s$ with <a id="eq:scalar-precision"></a>$$\label{eq:scalar-precision}
|\widetilde C_s-C_s|\le\delta_s/64.$$ Polynomially many square-root approximations suffice, since $C_s$ has polynomial magnitude and $\delta_s$ has an inverse-polynomial lower bound. Define the rational affine map <a id="eq:affine-map"></a>$$\label{eq:affine-map}
\widetilde F(z)=-mZ_*^2/2+Z_*\alpha(z-\widetilde C_s),$$ and set <a id="eq:output-thresholds"></a>$$\label{eq:output-thresholds}
a=\widetilde F(a_s+\delta_s/4),
\qquad b=\widetilde F(b_s-\delta_s/4).$$ All the quantities in these formulas are rational and computable with polynomial bit cost. The possibly enormous common offset $-mZ_*^2/2$ is an exact rational with polynomial bit length.

By [eq:full-final-error,eq:scalar-precision] [eq:full-final-error](#eq:full-final-error), [eq:scalar-precision](#eq:scalar-precision), <a id="eq:rounded-affine-error"></a>$$\label{eq:rounded-affine-error}
|E_0-\widetilde F(E_{\rm in})|
\le \frac{5}{64}Z_*\alpha\delta_s
<\frac18Z_*\alpha\delta_s.$$ Since $\widetilde F$ has positive slope, a YES source instance satisfies $$E_0\le\widetilde F(a_s)+\tfrac18Z_*\alpha\delta_s
\le a-\tfrac18Z_*\alpha\delta_s<a,$$ and a NO source instance satisfies $$E_0\ge\widetilde F(b_s)-\tfrac18Z_*\alpha\delta_s
\ge b+\tfrac18Z_*\alpha\delta_s>b.$$ These inequalities concern the exact continuum spectral infimum by <a href="#prop:continuum-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuum-simulation">28</a>, rather than only a projected eigenvalue.

<a id="eq:output-gap"></a>
The threshold separation is exactly $$\label{eq:output-gap}
b-a=\tfrac12 Z_*\alpha\delta_s.$$ The reciprocal of $\alpha\delta_s$ is polynomially bounded in $n$, whereas $Z_*\ge e^D/2$. Increasing the same fixed scale constant if necessary makes <a href="#eq:output-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:output-gap">[eq:output-gap]</a> at least one for every input. Since the output length $L\ge1$, this implies $b-a\ge L^{-1}$ without any circular estimate involving $L$.

We have constructed a legal nuclear instance in deterministic polynomial time, preserving YES and NO and permitting all spin sectors and all continuum states. This proves <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

      "caption": "The contact construction in the local $(ℓ,ζ)$ plane, with $h=\\sqrt3/2$ and $w_e=\\sqrt\\Delta\\,v_e$. Filled vertices are original spins and open vertices are ancillas. Every solid segment has length one; thick segments carry weight $Δ$ and thin segments the indicated weak weight. The dashed segment in (c) is an additional unit contact, with weight zero in $H_{+}$; it receives only the small positive hopping floor in the Hubbard model.       "witness_description": "Three subfigures showing gadget configurations:\n- (a) labeled \"(a) $J_e < 0$\": Nodes $L$ at $(0, 0)$ and $R$ at $(2, 0)$ are filled circles; node $A_e$ at $(1, 0)$ is an open circle along the bottom horizontal line. Node $B_e$ at $(1, 1)$ is an open circle directly above $A_e$. A thick vertical line segment connects $A_e$ and $B_e$ labeled $\\Delta$. Thin line segments connect $L$ to $B_e$ (labeled $w_e$) and $B_e$ to $R$ (labeled $w_e$). Solid horizontal line segments connect $L$ to $A_e$ and $A_e$ to $R$.\n- (b) labeled \"(b) $J_e > 0$\": Nodes $L$ at $(0, 0)$ and $R$ at $(2, 0)$ are filled circles on the horizontal axis. Above them are open circle nodes $A_e$ at $(1/2, h)$ and $B_e$ at $(3/2, h)$. A thin segment connects $L$ to $A_e$ labeled $w_e$. A thick horizontal segment connects $A_e$ to $B_e$ labeled $\\Delta$. A thin segment connects $B_e$ to $R$ labeled $w_e$.\n- (c) labeled \"(c) Successive positive bonds\": Along the baseline are filled circles $L$, $R = L'$, and $R'$. Above at vertical height $h$ (marked with a vertical dimension indicator labeled $h$) are four open circles: $A_e$, $B_e$, $A_f$, and $B_f$. Segments connect $L$ to $A_e$ (thin), $A_e$ to $B_e$ (thick), $B_e$ to $R = L'$ (thin), $R = L'$ to $A_f$ (thin), $A_f$ to $B_f$ (thick), and $B_f$ to $R'$ (thin). A horizontal dashed segment connects $B_e$ and $A_f$, labeled \"extra contact\"."
